iii 
About This Report 
The One Big Beautiful Bill Act, which contains a broad variety of tax and spending policies and 
was signed into law in July 2025, contains several provisions that affect Medicaid, the health insurance 
program for low-income individuals. These provisions could have broad implications for state 
Medicaid budgets and coverage. In this report, we describe customized approaches that we developed 
by combining publicly available data, published literature, state and federal agency reports, and other 
sources of information to build state-level estimates of budget impact and enrollment changes 
associated with several key Medicaid provisions. 
RAND Health 
This work was conducted in the Financing and Organization Program of RAND Health, a 
division of RAND that strengthens health care systems and the societal conditions that support 
health. For more information, visit www.rand.org/health or email Health@rand.org.

iv 
Summary 
Issue 
The One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) is a far-reaching budget reconciliation package (Pub. L. 
119-21, 2025). OBBBA contains many provisions that affect Medicaid, the state and federally 
financed health insurance program for people with low incomes or disabilities. These changes could 
have implications for financing, eligibility, and access to care. Although the Congressional Budget 
Office (CBO) has scored each provision (CBO, 2025c), CBO’s focus is on the federal budgetary and 
coverage implications. We built estimates of the expected impacts of a set of key provisions on 
Medicaid and state budgets, both provision by provision and overall. We also estimated the expected 
effect on Medicaid enrollment. A key contribution of this analysis is that we developed estimates for 
each state, typically drawing on state-specific data, documentation, and other materials. 
Approach  
We modeled the impacts of the following provisions (drawn from the law’s sections as indicated): 
• Sec. 71103 (address verification): This section requires states to collect enrollee addresses, 
an approach intended to reduce duplicative enrollment across states. 
• Sec. 71106 (good faith waiver): This section limits the federal government’s ability to waive 
recoupment of funds provided for certain types of erroneous Medicaid payments, sometimes 
known as a “good faith” waiver. 
• Sec. 71107 (redetermination): This section requires states to conduct eligibility 
redeterminations for Medicaid expansion adults every six months (as opposed to the previous 
requirement of every 12 months). 
• Sec. 71109 (eligibility restrictions for immigrants): This section limits Medicaid eligibility 
for lawfully present immigrants to green card holders and certain other groups. Refugees and 
asylees are no longer eligible. 
• Sec. 71110 (emergency Medicaid): This section reduces federal matching assistance 
percentage (FMAP) contributions for certain Medicaid-ineligible immigrants who seek 
emergency care. 
• Secs. 71115 and 71117 (provider taxes): These sections limit the ability of states to use 
provider taxes (i.e., taxes for which at least 85 percent of revenue comes from health care 
providers [Social Security Administration, undated]) to increase FMAP claims and impose 
greater restrictions on Medicaid-expansion states than on non-expansion ones. This provision 
curtails a common practice of imposing provider taxes alongside Medicaid payment increases, 
drawing down FMAP on the payment increases and simultaneously benefiting both states and 
providers at the expense of the federal government.

v 
• Sec. 71116 (state-directed payments [SDPs]): In certain circumstances, states can require 
Medicaid managed care plans to pay providers a specified amount. Previously, these payments 
could be as high as the average commercial rate; this section of OBBBA restricts payments for 
certain provider categories to 100 percent of average Medicare rates for expansion states and 
to 110 percent for non-expansion states. 
• Sec. 71119 (work requirements): With some exceptions, this section of the law requires 
non-disabled adults to be working, looking for work, or volunteering to qualify for Medicaid. 
• Sec. 71120 (cost-sharing): This section requires states to impose cost-sharing of up to $35 
for certain health care services for non-disabled adults. 
• Sec. 71121 (home- and community-based services [HCBS]): This section expands states’ 
ability to offer HCBS via Section 1915(c) waivers. Currently, Section 1915(c) of the Social 
Security Act allows states to waive certain Medicaid requirements in order to provide HCBS 
services to people who require an institutional level of care (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid 
Services, [CMS], undated-c). This provision allows states to offer HCBS to people who do 
not need an institutional level of care if waivers meet other necessary criteria. 
• Sec. 71401 (Rural Health Transformation Program [RHTP]): This section provides $50 
billion in funding for certain activities, such as promoting healthy behavior and paying for 
health care services. The funding is available to all states, though some of the criteria used to 
allocate the funding favor rural states. 
For ten of the 12 provisions that we modeled, we created customized approaches. For each 
approach, we relied on publicly available data on Medicaid enrollment and spending at the state level 
available from the Data.Medicaid.gov website (CMS, 2025j) and from the Medicaid and CHIP 
Payment and Access Commission (undated-a). We then used enrollment and spending projections 
from the National Health Expenditure Accounts (CMS, 2025d) to project estimates to future years. 
For many of the analyses, we used data from such sources as the American Community Survey (ACS) 
(U.S. Census Bureau, 2025), the Medicaid Budget and Expenditure System (MBES) (CMS, undated-
b), and CMS’s Healthcare Cost Reporting and Information System (CMS, 2025h). We also reviewed 
state laws and documentation, reports published by state or federal agencies, aggregated data sources, 
and academic literature. For the remaining two provisions, we allocated the CBO estimates to states 
(CBO, 2025c). 
Key Findings 
We estimate that impacts of the provisions will vary by state, but most states will see reductions to 
their state Medicaid budgets as a result of the OBBBA provisions that we studied. Key findings from 
our analyses include the following:  
• In total, state Medicaid budgets will be reduced by $664 billion over the 2025–2034 period.1 
State general funds will be reduced by $87 billion over the same period. 
 
1 The reduction to state Medicaid budgets represents the total loss from both reduced federal funding and reduced fundings from 
the state general fund.

vi 
• The federal savings amount to $714 billion, similar to the $709 billion in federal savings for 
these provisions projected by CBO (2025c). 
• Expansion states with substantial use of SDPs and provider taxes—Arizona, Iowa, and 
Nevada—stand to see reductions of more than 15 percent of their Medicaid budgets (a 
combined impact of reduced federal and state spending). California and New York similarly 
have substantial use of SDPs and provider taxes and see the largest dollar-value reductions to 
Medicaid budgets, on the order of $112 billion and $63 billion, respectively. 
• As a non-expansion state without substantial use of SDPs or provider taxes, Florida sees 
minimal budgetary impacts of the OBBBA provisions we studied (a <0.5-percent change to its 
Medicaid budget). Some expansion states, such as North Dakota and Nebraska, that similarly 
do not rely heavily on SDPs and provider taxes will also see minimal budgetary impacts of the 
provisions we studied because the expected gains from the RHTP are likely to offset the 
majority of the losses from other provisions. 
• A few states, such as Wyoming and South Dakota, will see increases in Medicaid budgets of 
close to 10 percent and 2 percent respectively; these states have small populations (and 
therefore smaller Medicaid budgets overall), so they stand to gain substantially in percentage 
terms from the RHTP and see little to no impact from other provisions. 
• There is variation across states in which provisions have the greatest budgetary impact (e.g., 
Michigan, California, and Colorado all see the largest impacts from provider taxes; Texas, 
Tennessee, and Mississippi are more affected by SDPs). 
• We estimate a joint impact across the provisions we modeled of 7.6 million fewer Medicaid 
enrollees in 2034. 
Policy Implications 
• Many states will see substantial reductions to Medicaid budgets, with 20 states expected to see 
reductions of 5 percent or more. 
• Some of the policies, such as work requirements and more-frequent redetermination, are 
expected to have direct impacts on Medicaid enrollment by adding eligibility criteria and 
increasing administrative requirements to remain enrolled. These policies reduce Medicaid 
spending for both states and the federal government.  
• Other provisions related to provider taxes, emergency FMAP, and the good faith waiver for 
erroneous payments essentially reduce federal funding without inherently reducing the size of 
the Medicaid program, requiring states to either find other funding sources to bridge the gap 
or reduce Medicaid spending by reducing eligibility, covered services, provider payment rates, 
or some combination of those. 
• There could be broader impacts of OBBBA provisions on access to care and uncompensated 
care that states might wish to address; the RHTP could at least partially address some of these 
issues, although the impact will vary substantially across states, with smaller states standing to 
gain more than larger states.

vii

1 
Chapter 1 
Introduction 
On July 4, 2025, President Donald Trump signed Public Law 119-21 (Pub. L. 119-21, 2025), a 
massive budget reconciliation package with implications for many sectors of the U.S. economy. The 
law, commonly referred to as the One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA), contains a series of policy 
changes that aim to reduce federal spending and improve program integrity in Medicaid, the nation’s 
state-funded and federally funded health program for people with low incomes and disabilities. 
Broadly, these Medicaid changes impose work requirements on non-disabled adults, increase the 
frequency and stringency of eligibility determinations and redeterminations, limit spending on 
immigrants, and tighten provisions that have historically enabled states to increase their federal 
funding. Although the law also includes some new Medicaid spending, notably a $50 billion Rural 
Health Transformation Program (RHTP), the Congressional Budget Office (CBO) estimates that all 
of the Medicaid provisions in OBBBA will, in net, reduce federal Medicaid spending by $911 billion 
between 2025 and 2034 (Euhus et al., 2025). CBO further estimates that up to 10 million people 
could lose coverage as a result of these changes, combined with additional changes to the Affordable 
Care Act’s (ACA’s) Marketplaces (CBO, 2025a). 
Some state policymakers have expressed concern about how the cuts to the Medicaid program will 
affect their budgets (McCarthy and Carney, 2025; NY State of Health, 2025; State of Rhode Island, 
2025). However, because the CBO score does not provide state-specific impact estimates, the 
magnitude of the impact for any given state is uncertain. In this report, we describe our detailed, state-
level estimates of the budgetary effects of many of OBBBA’s key Medicaid provisions for each state. 
We also estimated state-specific coverage changes resulting from these provisions. Although prior 
studies have allocated the CBO’s estimate across states (Euhus et al., 2025), we developed most of 
these estimates independently from the CBO scores, often using state-specific source material and 
data. This approach enabled us to capture such nuances as state-specific Medicaid provider tax rates 
and the size of the immigrant population. These estimates might be of interest to state policymakers 
or other stakeholders as they consider the needed Medicaid and state budget changes that will arise as 
OBBBA provisions take effect along with changes in health insurance coverage within their states. 
Description of OBBBA Provisions 
We modeled the following 12 Medicaid provisions that were in the final version of OBBBA 
(drawn from sections of Pub. L. 119-21 [2025] as indicated): 
• Sec. 71103 (address verification): This section requires states to verify enrollee addresses, an 
approach intended to reduce duplicative enrollment across states.

2 
• Sec. 71106 (good faith waiver): This section limits the federal government’s ability to waive 
recoupment of funds provided for certain types of erroneous Medicaid payments, sometimes 
known as a “good faith” waiver. 
• Sec. 71107 (redetermination): This section requires states to conduct eligibility 
redeterminations for Medicaid-expansion adults every six months (as opposed to the previous 
requirement of every 12 months). 
• Sec. 71109 (eligibility restrictions for immigrants): This section limits Medicaid eligibility 
for lawfully present immigrants to green card holders and certain other groups. Refugees and 
asylees are no longer eligible. 
• Sec. 71110 (emergency Medicaid): This section reduces federal matching assistance 
percentage (FMAP) contributions for certain Medicaid-ineligible immigrants who seek 
emergency care. Under the Emergency Medical Treatment and Labor Act (EMTALA), 
hospitals must provide emergency care regardless of a patient’s insurance or immigration 
status, and Medicaid can reimburse some of these costs. Previously, the federal contribution 
for this care reflected what the state would have received had the immigrant been a U.S. 
citizen, including 90-percent FMAP for Medicaid expansion adults. OBBBA limits the 
FMAP recovery to the regular FMAP, which is lower than the expansion FMAP. 
• Secs. 71115 and 71117 (provider taxes): These sections limit the ability of states to use 
provider taxes (i.e., taxes for which at least 85 percent of revenue comes from health care 
providers [Social Security Administration, undated]) to increase FMAP claims and impose 
greater restrictions on Medicaid-expansion states than on non-expansion ones. This provision 
curtails a common practice of imposing provider taxes alongside Medicaid payment increases, 
drawing down FMAP on the payment increases and simultaneously benefiting both states and 
providers at the expense of the federal government.  
• Sec. 71116 (state-directed payments [SDPs]): In certain circumstances, states can require 
Medicaid managed care plans to pay providers a specified amount. Previously, these payments 
could be as high as the average commercial rate (ACR); this section of OBBBA restricts the 
payments for inpatient care, outpatient care, services provided at an academic medical center 
(AMC), and nursing facility services to 100 percent of average Medicare rates for expansion 
states and to 110 percent for non-expansion states. 
• Sec. 71119 (work requirements): With some exceptions, this section of the law requires 
non-disabled adults to be working, looking for work, or volunteering to qualify for Medicaid. 
• Sec. 71120 (cost-sharing): This section requires states to impose cost-sharing of up to $35 
for certain health care services for non-disabled adults. 
• Sec. 71121 (home- and community-based services [HCBS]): This section expands states’ 
ability to offer HCBS via 1915(c) waivers. Currently, Section 1915(c) of the Social Security 
Act allows states to waive certain Medicaid requirements in order to provide HCBS services to 
people who require an institutional level of care (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services 
[CMS], undated-c). Specifically, this provision allows states to offer HCBS to people who do 
not need an institutional level of care if waivers meet other necessary criteria.

3 
• Sec. 71401 (RHTP): This section provides $50 billion in funding for certain activities, such 
as promoting healthy behavior and paying for health care services. The funding is available to 
all states, though some of the criteria used to allocate the funding favor rural states. 
We did not model provisions that (1) were entirely dependent on prediction of state behavior 
(e.g., Sec. 71114, which eliminates the temporary FMAP bump for states that expand Medicaid; this 
would require us to predict which states might expand Medicaid or might have expanded in absence of 
this provision but will not because of it), (2) were estimated by CBO to reduce the federal deficit by 
less than $5 billion over ten years (e.g., Sec. 71112, which limits retroactive Medicaid coverage; this 
provision is estimated to save $4.2 billion over the 2025–2034 period), or (3) prevented the 
implementation of federal final rules that had not yet taken effect (e.g., Sec. 71101 on the Eligibility 
and Enrollment Final Rule).  
Methodology 
A key contribution of this analysis is that we developed estimates for each state, typically drawing 
on state-specific data, documentation, and other materials. Some of the data sources that we used were 
the American Community Survey (ACS) (U.S. Census Bureau, 2025), the Medicaid Budget and 
Expenditure System (MBES) (CMS, undated-b), the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services’ 
(CMS’s) Healthcare Cost Reporting and Information System (HCRIS) (CMS, 2025h), and data 
available through the Data.Medicaid.gov website (CMS, 2025j) and Medicaid and CHIP Payment 
and Access Commission (undated-a). In addition, we consulted state laws, reports published by 
federal and state agencies, and academic literature. (This material can be found in an annex to this 
report, available at www.rand.org/t/RRA4098-1.) We derived unique, state-specific estimates for ten 
of the 12 provisions. For the remaining two provisions—the good faith waiver reduction and the 
HCBS waiver expansion—we allocated CBO’s estimate across states (CBO, 2025c). A detailed 
discussion of the methodology for each provision can be found in Appendix A. To develop the ten-
year forecast, we used health care cost and Medicaid population growth projections from the National 
Health Expenditure Accounts (NHEA) (CMS, 2025d).  
Six of the provisions that we considered would result in a loss of coverage, either because the 
policy directly reduces eligibility (work requirements, redeterminations, and restrictions on immigrant 
coverage) or because the policy negatively affects the state’s budget, which in turn could lead states to 
reduce eligibility (provider taxes, reductions in emergency FMAP, and limitations on the good faith 
waiver). For the latter cases, we followed CBO (2025c) in assuming that states make up one-half of 
the budget deficit by reducing Medicaid spending and the other half by leveraging the general fund. 
We further assumed that states would achieve one-third of the necessary cuts to Medicaid spending 
by reducing enrollment, with the other two-thirds reflecting changes in provider payment and service 
provision. When we report enrollment reductions, we focus on coverage losses for 2034 because all of 
OBBBA’s policies will be fully implemented by that point.

4 
Chapter 2 
Overall Impacts of Key Provisions 
We estimated the impacts of several of OBBBA’s key Medicaid provisions at the state level, 
incorporating both states’ possible responses to the provisions and overlapping effects of some of the 
provisions.2 We considered changes in both spending and enrollment. For spending, we considered 
three affected budgets: the state Medicaid budget, the state general fund, and the federal government, 
where the state Medicaid budget receives funding for an enrollee from both the federal government 
(equal to the FMAP for that state and enrollee type times the total funding amount) and the state 
general fund (equal to the difference between the federal contribution and the total funding amount). 
We also note two types of provisions in OBBBA: (1) those that reduce spending and also inherently 
reduce the need for that funding and (2) those that reduce federal funding but do not inherently 
reduce the need for funding. For example, a provision such as Medicaid work requirements functions 
by reducing Medicaid enrollment. In this case, the state Medicaid budget would lose funding for the 
disenrolled individuals while the state general fund and the federal government would see savings 
equal to their share of the funding for the disenrolled individuals. There would not be a direct need for 
the state general fund to replace the funding that was removed from the state Medicaid budget. In 
contrast, states use provider taxes to fund higher Medicaid spending, which results in increased 
FMAP. Limits on taxes will strain the state’s general fund, which could require states to respond by 
cutting Medicaid budgets, reducing both federal and state outlays. We detail our methodological 
approach in Appendix A. 
In total, we estimate that state Medicaid budgets will be reduced by $664 billion in funding 
through 2034 as a result of the provisions that we studied. State general funds would lose $87 billion 
because of two generally opposing effects: (1) the savings that are realized because of decreased 
Medicaid enrollment and provider payment rates and (2) the loss of funds as a result of lower provider 
taxes. Overall, we estimate that the federal government would realize $714 billion in savings,3 similar 
to the $709 billion in federal savings estimated by the CBO (2025c) for the same provisions. After 
accounting for potential overlap in impacts across provisions, we expect a decrease in Medicaid 
enrollment nationally of 7.6 million enrollees in 2034. 
The findings we present in this section account for the potential overlap of impacts across 
provisions. For example, the enrollment impacts of the work requirements and redetermination 
provisions are likely to affect some of the same people. Other provisions could offset one another; for 
 
2 A detailed description of our approach is included in Appendix A. 
3 In general, for provisions that reduce enrollment (e.g., work requirements, redetermination), the reduction in the state Medicaid 
budget is equal to the sum of the savings to the federal government and to the state general fund. For provisions that reduce 
federal funding (e.g., emergency FMAP, good faith waiver), the reductions to the state general fund and to the state Medicaid 
budget sum to the savings to the federal government. The exception is for provider taxes, which further affects non-Medicaid 
providers.

5 
example, state general funds might recognize savings as a result of decreased SDPs but require 
increased outflows as a result of the provider tax provision. 
We detail the state- and provision-level findings on state Medicaid budgets, state general funds, 
and enrollment in Appendix B. 
Overall Impacts by Provision 
We considered the relative impacts of each of the provisions we studied. In general, we expect 
expansion states (affected by work requirements, more-frequent redeterminations, and cost-sharing 
provisions and more affected by SDP and provider tax provisions), those with use of SDPs, and those 
with use of provider taxes to be more affected by the OBBBA provisions we studied than non-
expansion states will be. Table 2.1 summarizes the Medicaid expansion status, use of SDPs, and use of 
provider taxes for each state as of 2025.

7 
Budgetary Impacts by Provision 
Figure 2.1 summarizes the combined impact across states and years (2025–2034) of each 
provision on state budgets (the sum of the impact on Medicaid and state general funds). The 
provisions with the largest impacts are those related to Medicaid financing or eligibility. The 
implementation of Medicaid work requirements and more-frequent redetermination for the expansion 
population represents a reduction to state budgets (sum of Medicaid budget impact and general fund 
impact) of nearly $350 billion through 2034 because of decreased enrollment. This represents a $388 
billion reduction to state Medicaid budgets and a $40 billion increase to state general funds. Similarly, 
more-frequent redetermination would lead to reductions of nearly $70 billion, with reductions of $77 
billion to state Medicaid budgets but savings of nearly $8 billion to state general funds. We expect that 
these provisions would lead to decreased enrollment for two reasons: (1) Some people will disenroll 
because they do not meet eligibility requirements, and (2) some people will meet the eligibility 
requirements but become disenrolled because of the administrative burden of demonstrating 
compliance. These enrollment provisions represent a substantial loss to state Medicaid budgets 
because the federal government finances 90 percent of Medicaid costs for Medicaid expansion adults, 
which is much higher than the standard FMAP for other Medicaid populations. The provisions that 
affect Medicaid financing by changing rules related to provider taxes and SDPs also lead to substantial 
budgetary losses of $278 billion and $169 billion, respectively. We note that these are provision-level 
estimates, which do not account for overlap or interaction across provisions. 
The provisions related to enrollee address verification, immigrant eligibility, and cost-sharing for a 
subset of the expansion population all stand to have relatively smaller impacts of less than $10 billion 
each over the 2025–2034 period because they affect a smaller proportion of the Medicaid population 
and because provisions will be phased in during this period. The RHTP and the provision related to 
HCBS waivers are the only two provisions that will increase funding to states.

8

Enrollment Impacts by Provision 
Work requirements, more-frequent redeterminations, and changes to immigrant eligibility 
directly affect enrollment because these provisions require states to tighten eligibility rules. 
Furthermore, we expect that changes to good faith waivers, provider taxes, and emergency FMAP will 
have indirect effects on enrollment because of reductions in federal funding that could lead states to 
reduce enrollment as a way of addressing budgetary shortfalls. The remaining provisions either achieve 
savings for both states and the federal government without restricting enrollment (address verification, 
SDPs, cost-sharing) or represent transfers of funding from the federal government to states (HCBS 
waivers, RHTP).  
Figure 2.2 shows the projected change in Medicaid enrollment by provision, focusing on the six 
provisions that we expect to affect enrollment. Work requirements have the largest impact, reducing 
enrollment by 5.3 million in 2034, followed by provider taxes (1.5 million fewer enrollees) and 
redeterminations (0.9 million fewer enrollees). These numbers shown in the figure are not 
deduplicated, meaning that they estimate the expected total enrollment change if each provision were 
implemented in isolation. However, some provisions will affect the same people; for example, some

9 
people who might lose coverage because of more-frequent redeterminations might also be unable to 
meet the work requirements. After deduplication, we estimate that there will be 7.6 million fewer 
Medicaid enrollees in 2034 because of the OBBBA provisions that we studied.

Overall Impacts by State 
Impacts on Medicaid Budgets, by State 
We summarize the overall impact of the 12 provisions we studied on state Medicaid budgets 
across the 2025–2034 period by state in Figure 2.3. There is substantial state-to-state variation in the 
relative impact of the provisions. Arizona, Iowa, and Nevada stand to see the largest Medicaid budget 
reductions, at more than 15 percent of the total Medicaid budget across the period we studied. All 
three states are Medicaid expansion states and heavily reliant on SDPs; therefore, they see large 
funding losses from the work requirements and SDP provisions. Other states—such as Florida, 
North Dakota, and Nebraska—see close to no impact of the provisions we studied. As a non-
expansion state, Florida is shielded from many of the provisions. It sees nearly equal positive budget 
impacts from the RHTP and negative budget impacts from other provisions, leading to a neutral 
impact across provisions. North Dakota and Nebraska are expansion states—but, as less populous 
states, they stand to gain more proportionally from the RHTP, offsetting losses from other

Impacts on State General Funds, by State 
In Figure 2.4, we summarize the impact of the provisions we studied on state general funds, 
relative to the Medicaid budget (CMS, 2025c). Many of the OBBBA Medicaid provisions (Pub. L. 
119-21, 2025) we studied that will reduce Medicaid spending (i.e., work requirements, SDPs, 
redeterminations, address verification, immigrant eligibility, and cost-sharing) will result in savings to 
state general funds. The remaining provisions reduce federal funding to state Medicaid budgets 
without reducing the need for those funds; these provisions would require additional funding from 
state general funds to bridge the gap. Such states as Tennessee, Mississippi, Oklahoma, and Kentucky 
are all expected to see savings to their general funds of more than 2 percent, relative to Medicaid 
budgets. California, on the other hand, is expected to see a reduction to its state general fund of more 
than 6 percent.

Impacts on Enrollment, by State 
Figure 2.5 shows the estimated percentage change in Medicaid enrollment for each state for 2034. 
Because these effects are dominated by the work requirements provision, states with the largest 
changes are expansion states with relatively high enrollment of non-disabled adults. We estimate that 
West Virginia, Oregon, New Mexico, and the District of Columbia would all experience declines in 
Medicaid enrollment of more than 20 percent. In contrast, non-expansion states would experience 
little change in enrollment because work requirements (and several other provisions) do not come into 
play for these states. The exception is Wisconsin, which (even though it is a non-expansion state) is 
affected by work requirements because it covers non-disabled adults with incomes below 100 percent 
of FPL (Wisconsin Department of Health Services, 2025a).

Overall Impacts by State and Provision 
We also considered the relative impact of the provisions (across 2025–2034) by state. In Figure 
2.6, we display the relative importance of each provision as it is expected to affect state budgets. For 
the purposes of the figure, we considered the absolute value of the impact of each provision and 
combined the emergency FMAP, address verification, good faith waiver, immigrant eligibility, cost-
sharing, and HCBS waiver provisions into one category under “other” because of their smaller overall 
impact. As the figure illustrates, there is substantial variation across states regarding which provisions 
have the greatest impact. Such states as Tennessee, Texas, Georgia, South Carolina, and Mississippi 
are most affected by the changes to SDPs because they are not highly reliant on provider taxes; as non-
expansion states, they are not affected by provisions that target the expansion population. Other 
states, such as California and Michigan, are most affected by the provider tax provision, followed by 
Medicaid work requirements. Several less populous states (particularly non-expansion states, such as 
Wyoming and Alabama) are not likely to affected by much other than the RHTP.

Chapter 3 
Provision-by-Provision Findings 
In addition to modeling the overall impacts of key Medicaid provisions in OBBBA (Pub. L. 119-
21, 2025), we also estimated the impacts of each provision individually (Boxes 3.1–3.11). In this 
chapter, we describe our provision-by-provision findings; we note that these impacts are the individual 
impacts of each provision as though they happened alone and do not account for overlap with other 
provisions. (The overall impacts described in the previous chapter do take such impacts into account.) 
Work Requirements 
Under OBBBA, starting in 2027, 
adults enrolled in Medicaid through the 
Medicaid expansion must demonstrate 
community engagement (commonly 
referred to as work requirements) to 
qualify for or maintain their coverage. A 
person can meet this requirement in any 
given month by working at least 80 
hours, completing 80 hours of 
community service, participating in a 
work program, being enrolled in school 
or training at least half-time, combining any of these activities to reach 80 hours, or earning the 
equivalent of 80 hours at minimum wage. 
Not all expansion adults are subject to this rule. Exempt categories include children under 19, 
people enrolled in or eligible for Medicare (Parts A or B), American Indians and Alaska Natives, 
veterans with total disability ratings, those who are medically frail or have serious or complex medical 
conditions, caregivers for children or disabled family members, and a few additional smaller groups 
(Pub. L. 119-21, 2025).  
To assess the legislation’s effects on Medicaid enrollment and spending, we applied the full set of 
compliance and exemption criteria to each state’s population. Exemption criteria identify individuals 
who are not subject to the work requirement because they fall into specified categorical groups 
(referred to as “exceptions” or “exclusions” in OBBBA [Pub. L. 119-21, 2025]). Compliance criteria 
define the conditions under which non-exempt individuals are considered to have satisfied the work 
requirements. After accounting for all individuals who meet the requirements or qualify for an 
exemption, approximately 3.7 million adults remain who are neither exempt nor compliant and 
therefore face a potential loss of Medicaid coverage. To illustrate how this number was derived, Table 
3.1 presents the sequential application of compliance and exemption criteria. The top row corresponds

are aggregated into a general “Other” category.

After excluding the relatively small subset of individuals with potential access to employer-
sponsored insurance and those who could work more hours to retain eligibility, we estimate that 
approximately 3.5 million expansion adults—representing 17.7 percent of the expansion population—
will lose Medicaid coverage. The majority of these individuals are people who are either not seeking 
employment or have been unemployed for extended periods. Compared with those who retain 
coverage, this group is more likely to be single, childless, and living in households with elderly parents.  
However, individuals who fail to meet the work requirements are not the only group expected to 
lose Medicaid coverage. Evidence from states that implemented Medicaid work requirements (Harker, 
2023; Johnson et al., 2025; Karpman, Haley, and Kenney, 2025; Musumeci, Rudowitz, and Lyons, 
2018; Sommers et al., 2020), particularly Arkansas, indicates that many coverage losses occurred 
among individuals who were actually compliant or exempt. For these individuals, disenrollment was 
driven by administrative and informational barriers—such as lack of awareness, confusion about 
reporting rules, or difficulties completing paperwork—rather than failure to meet work requirements. 
Accordingly, we estimate that approximately 10 percent of individuals who are otherwise compliant 
with, or exempt from, the work requirements will lose Medicaid coverage each year because of 
administrative and informational barriers. As a result, the total number of individuals disenrolled from 
Medicaid as a consequence of the work-requirements provision is projected to reach 5.2 million by 
2034. 
The share of Medicaid expansion adults who become uninsured varies substantially across states, 
reflecting differences not only in labor force participation and long-term unemployment but also in the

16 
magnitude of administrative barriers. In such states as Alaska, North Carolina, and New Hampshire, 
this share is as low as 17 percent, whereas in such states as Utah, Maryland, and New Mexico, it rises 
to 40 percent. Given that the FMAP for the population affected by work requirements is 90 percent 
(Pub. L. 111-148, 2010)—with the exception of Wisconsin, which has a lower rate (Wisconsin 
Department of Health Services, 2025b)—projected Medicaid disenrollment has a relatively modest 
effect on state general funds. Assuming full implementation of the provision by the end of 2029, state 
general funds are projected to increase by approximately $40 billion over the 2027–2034 period; the 
overall state Medicaid budget would decrease by $388 billion, resulting in a corresponding increase of 
roughly $348 billion in the federal budget.  
Regarding the increase in the state general fund as a saving to the state, we estimate that expansion 
states in 2027 would save, on average, approximately $967 for each Medicaid enrollee who loses 
coverage. Many of these enrollees could become uninsured as a result.4 However, there is considerable 
variation across states, reflecting differences in Medicaid personal expenditures (KFF, undated-b). At 
the lower end of the distribution, such states as North Carolina, Colorado, and Oklahoma realize 
savings just above $600 per disenrollee. In contrast, such states as Nebraska, Minnesota, and North 
Dakota are at the upper end, with savings per disenrollee ranging from $1,300 to $1,400. Wisconsin is 
a notable outlier, with savings of $2,500 per disenrollee, driven by the comparatively lower FMAP 
applied to its expansion population. 
Provider Taxes 
States can use revenue generated from 
health care provider taxes to finance the state 
share of Medicaid spending (Mitchell et al., 
2025). When combined with policies that 
increase Medicaid spending, such as increases 
in provider payment or eligibility expansions, 
these taxes can be used to draw down 
additional federal matching funds. For 
instance, a state could levy a provider tax while 
increasing Medicaid payment rates by the 
same amount. This increases Medicaid 
spending while leaving providers no worse off. 
Because the federal government pays a portion of Medicaid spending, there is a net gain in state funds, 
which can be spent on Medicaid or transferred to the general fund. 
OBBBA (Pub. L. 119-21, 2025) limits states’ ability to draw down additional federal Medicaid 
funding through provider taxes in three ways. First, it incentivizes expansion states to reduce their 
taxes by gradually lowering the hold harmless threshold (the point at which taxes trigger federal 
review) from 6 percent in 2027 to 3.5 percent in 2032. Second, it prohibits states from imposing new 
 
4 States might bear some of the budgetary burden of individuals losing Medicaid coverage because of this and other provisions if 
the individuals become uninsured and rely instead on uncompensated care pools. 
Box 3.2. Key Impacts of the  
Provider Tax Provision 
Number of states affected

State Medicaid budget 
−$139 billion

−$139 billion 
Federal budget 
+$241 billion 
2034 enrollment impact 
−1.5 million

17 
provider taxes or increasing rates of existing taxes. Third, it grants CMS greater discretion to deny 
waivers when a tax falls disproportionately on Medicaid providers. CMS has indicated it intends to 
use this authority to withhold waivers for eight taxes in seven states. 
We estimate that OBBBA’s provider tax provisions will reduce total state budgets (the sum of the 
Medicaid budget and the state general fund) by $278 billion between 2024 and 2034, assuming that 
states would stay within the hold harmless threshold. Of this reduction, $241.1 billion would come 
from reduced federal spending, and $36.8 billion would come from reductions in taxes on non-
Medicaid patients. We assume that states would address one-half of this reduction in revenue 
($138.9 billion) by leveraging their general funds. States would absorb the remaining revenue cuts by 
reducing spending on the Medicaid program. This could take place through reductions in enrollment, 
provider payment rates, or services. If we assume that states replace one-third of their reduced 
Medicaid budget through enrollment reductions (at the average FMAP for each state), we estimate 
that 1.5 million people would lose coverage in 2034. Coverage losses would be lower in earlier years 
because the policy phases in gradually.  
Figure 3.1 shows the total losses to state Medicaid and general fund budgets from 2025 to 2034 
that stem from the provider tax provisions as a percentage of Medicaid spending, broken down by tax 
type. States that have high tax rates or that lose their CMS waivers face the largest losses. California 
exemplifies this group, with expected losses of $152.2 billion, more than one-half the nationwide total, 
representing 9.1 percent of its Medicaid spending between 2025 and 2034. These losses are largely a 
result of CMS indicating that it will no longer grant waivers for the California managed care 
organization (MCO) tax (CMS, undated-d; CMS, 2025c), which previously allowed the state to 
impose tax rates of nearly 14 percent on Medicaid plans while effectively exempting non-Medicaid 
insurers. Using federal matching funds, this tax generated $7.6 billion in net revenue for the state 
annually while leaving its providers no worse off (Legislative Analyst’s Office, 2025). 
States with high FMAPs, such as West Virginia, also face substantial cuts. The impact of lost tax 
revenues is compounded in these states because most savings from reduced Medicaid spending accrue 
to the federal government, meaning the state must make larger cuts to balance its budget. 
Although every state except Alaska has at least one provider tax as of 2025 (CMS, undated-a), 
there are several exemptions in OBBBA that leave approximately one-half of states experiencing 
negligible budget changes. First, the ten states that did not expand Medicaid under the ACA were 
exempted from the decreased hold harmless threshold. This spared several states, such as Texas, that 
would otherwise have seen large reductions to their hospital tax revenues. Second, many states impose 
substantial taxes on nursing and intermediate care facilities (ICFs), but these provider classes were 
exempted from changes. Third, although many states tax ambulance providers, these revenues are 
typically so small that any reductions have no material effect on state budgets. Finally, broad-based 
taxes below 3.5 percent are unaffected by OBBBA provisions (if they existed prior to OBBBA 
passage).

State-Directed Payments 
Since 2016, CMS has allowed states to 
direct Medicaid MCOs to make payments to 
providers for the purposes of setting minimum 
or maximum fee schedules, making uniform 
increases to provider payment rates (similar to 
supplemental payments under Medicaid fee-
for-service [FFS]), or requiring participation 
in value-based payment models (Medicaid and 
CHIP Payment and Access Commission, 
2024). A 2024 final rule released by CMS 
sought to reduce the burden on states for 
implementing SDPs and to improve CMS 
oversight of such payments, stating particularly that the ACR for a given state, service, and provider 
class would serve as the upper limit on the payment amount that could be made for inpatient and 
outpatient hospital services, professional services at AMCs, and nursing facility services (CMS, 
2024a). CMS based this limit on precedent for previously approved SDPs, differences in the primary 
populations covered by Medicaid and Medicare that could make using Medicare payments as a 
benchmark challenging, and similar approvals of supplemental payments up to the ACR under the 
Medicaid FFS program.

When OBBBA (Pub. L. 119-21, 2025) was signed into law in July 2025, Sec. 71116 essentially 
repealed a final rule from President Joe Biden’s administration that set the maximum SDP for 
inpatient and outpatient services, professional services at AMCs, and nursing facility services at 100 
percent of the total published Medicare rate for Medicaid expansion states and at 110 percent of the 
total published Medicare rate for non-expansion states. This provision applies immediately to new 
SDP applications; for renewals of SDPs, the upper payment limit will be reduced by 10 percentage 
points each year until the new maximum is reached. 
As of 2025, a total of 31 states had at least one active SDP;5 of those, 24 states had at least one 
SDP that was affected by the OBBBA provision. On average, these SDPs were worth more than $3 
billion in 2025. We calculated the payment rate of each SDP item relative to Medicare rates;6 we 
found that rates varied from 36 percent to more than 300 percent of Medicare rates, with the median 
rate being nearly twice the Medicare rate. 
South Carolina and Nevada had the highest mean SDP rates relative to Medicare in 2025—at 
280 percent and 251 percent, respectively—so their SDPs will be reduced the most as a proportion of 
their total value. However, Texas and Tennessee will see the largest reductions to SDP amounts in 
dollar terms, at $19.7 billion and $15.9 billion, respectively, over the years 2025 to 2034. Tennessee, 
Mississippi, and South Carolina will see the biggest reductions in terms of percentage of state 
Medicaid budget (Figure 3.2). In total across all states and years, we estimate that this provision 
represents a reduction in Medicaid spending of $241 billion by 2034; $72 billion of this total will 
accrue to state general funds, and $169 billion will accrue to the federal government.

More-Frequent Redetermination 
Prior to the passing of OBBBA, states 
were required to conduct Medicaid eligibility 
redeterminations (during which the enrollee 
provides documentation of their continued 
eligibility for Medicaid) every 12 months but 
could conduct regular checks of available data 
to determine whether there were changes in 
beneficiaries’ incomes or other statuses that 
could affect eligibility (CMS, 2024a). 
Sec. 71119 of OBBBA requires states to 
conduct eligibility redeterminations every six 
months for the adult expansion population 
rather than every 12 months (Pub. L. 119-21, 2025). 
Eligibility redeterminations can result in disenrollment for one of two reasons: (1) The individual’s 
circumstances changed, leading to loss of eligibility, or (2) the individual loses coverage for procedural 
or administrative reasons (e.g., missing or incomplete documentation of eligibility or failure to respond 
to requests for information). We estimate that disenrollments resulting from eligibility loss are 
disenrollments that would have happened at the next redetermination anyway but might happen

21 
sooner when redetermination is conducted more frequently. Disenrollments for administrative or 
procedural reasons are likely to increase but might generally lead to more Medicaid churn (i.e., 
frequent disenrollment and re-enrollment from Medicaid, often because of administrative reasons). 
We found that the impact of this provision at the state level is dependent on the size of the 
expansion population relative to the Medicaid population as a whole and the per capita cost of the 
expansion population on average. Among Medicaid expansion states, those that are expected to see the 
largest proportion of disenrollments from their total Medicaid or Children’s Health Insurance 
Program (CHIP) populations as a result of more-frequent redetermination are Maryland, Montana, 
New Mexico, Utah, and Arizona (all more than 3 percent); states that will be affected the least are 
Kentucky, Michigan, North Carolina (all less than 0.5 percent). California and New York stand to 
lose the most enrollees in 2034, with California losing almost 90,000 enrollees and New York losing 
more than 130,000. Across all states, we estimate reductions to expected Medicaid enrollment of 
923,100 in 2034. 
We estimate that this provision would result in reduced spending (as a result of reduced 
enrollment) by state general funds of $7.7 billion and would result in reduced spending of $69.4 
billion by the federal government over the 2025–2034 period. States that are likely to be the most 
affected by this provision are Arizona, Delaware, and New Mexico; for these states, the reduced 
spending (sum of both federal and state) represents more than 2 percent of the total Medicaid budget. 
Conversely, the states with the smallest projected impact (excluding non-expansion states) are 
California, Michigan, and Oregon, which are all projected to have impacts on the total Medicaid 
budget of less than 0.4 percent.  
Federal Matching Assistance Percentage for Emergency 
Medicaid 
For certain immigrant groups, federal 
Medicaid funds can be used only to cover 
emergency medical services, not other care 
(Baumrucker and Kolker, 2025). This 
coverage is sometimes referred to as emergency 
Medicaid. Prior to OBBBA, expansion states 
could claim 90 percent FMAP for emergency 
Medicaid services provided to immigrants who 
would have qualified as expansion group 
adults if they had been citizens. Beginning in 
fiscal year 2027, Sec. 71110 of OBBBA 
prohibits states from claiming the 90-percent 
ACA matching rate for emergency Medicaid, requiring them to use their base FMAP instead (Pub. L. 
119-21, 2025). 
We estimate that this provision will reduce federal spending on Medicaid by $23.3 billion in the 
2025–2034 period. We assume that states will absorb one-half of this shortfall ($11.7 billion) 
through their general funds, and that the remaining $11.7 billion in cuts will be addressed by

reductions in state Medicaid spending. Although states cannot avoid providing emergency care to 
immigrants, they can reduce Medicaid spending through other means, such as by reducing payment 
rates, reducing the scope of services provided, reducing eligibility for optional groups, or making it 
more difficult for people to enroll. If we assume that one-third of the Medicaid cuts come from 
reduced enrollment, roughly 100,000 would lose coverage in 2034.  
The financial burden of this provision falls largely on California, which accounts for $21.2 billion, 
more than 90 percent of the total federal funding reduction. However, relative to overall Medicaid 
spending, the impact is modest. California would lose approximately 1.3 percent of its annual 
Medicaid spending, and no other state would lose more than 0.3 percent. Most states are unaffected 
because they were already using the base matching rate or had not expanded Medicaid. 
Address Verification 
OBBBA includes a provision that 
requires states to proactively collect reliable 
enrollee address information, with the goal 
of establishing a national system to share 
this information across states to prevent 
individuals from being simultaneously 
enrolled in more than one state Medicaid 
(Pub. L. 119-21, 2025). This provision 
requires states to collect the address 
information by 2027, and it requires the 
Secretary of Health and Human Services to 
establish the national system by October 
2029, meaning that the impacts of the policy largely occur in 2030 and beyond. 
We did not consider disenrollments that are due to multistate enrollments to be true reductions in 
Medicaid enrollment because those individuals would still maintain Medicaid coverage in their state of 
residence. Therefore, we estimate the enrollment impact to be zero. However, we translate the 
expected reductions in enrollment in each state to dollar amounts and estimate that, in total, state 
general funds will see savings of $4.4 billion through 2034, and the federal government will see savings 
of $8.4 billion. We expect that savings from multistate enrollment would occur in the Medicaid 
Managed Care program because managed care payments are made to MCOs regardless of actual care 
used, resulting in double payments if an individual is enrolled in two states. Therefore, states that do 
not have Medicaid MCOs (Alaska, Connecticut, and Vermont) would not see any impact from this 
provision. States that are expected to see the largest state savings as a result of this provision are 
Arizona, Delaware, Hawaii, and Kansas, which all have expected savings to their state general funds of 
more than 0.3 percent relative to their total Medicaid budgets over 2030 to 2034. States that have 
MCOs but are expected to see the least impact are Maine and South Dakota, with expected savings to 
those states of less than 0.001 percent of the total state Medicaid budget.

Good Faith Waiver Reduction for Erroneous Payments 
States sometimes make erroneous 
Medicaid payments—for example, for people 
who are not eligible or services that are not 
covered. The federal government can recover 
FMAP contributions made for erroneous 
payments of more than 3 percent of total 
spending (Code of Federal Regulations, Title 
42, Part 431, Subpart Q). However, the 
federal government can also waive FMAP 
recoupment for spending above the 3-percent 
threshold through a process referred to as a 
good faith waiver. Although OBBBA allows 
the good faith wavier to continue, it disallows waivers in excess of 3 percent of total spending if they 
are for improper payments related to eligibility. 
Following CBO (2025c), we estimate that the good faith waiver change will reduce federal 
spending by $7.6 billion between 2025 and 2034. We allocated this amount in proportion to state-
specific improper payments for eligibility errors uncovered during the Payment Error Rate 
Measurement (PERM) audit process (U.S. Government Accountability Office, 2025). Drawing on 
this methodology, we estimate that states with the largest good faith waiver federal payment 
reductions will be California ($2.3 billion reduction between 2025 and 2034), Illinois ($757 million), 
and Ohio ($683 million). However, for each of these states, the reduction represents a small share of 
federal Medicaid funding (less than 1 percent). 
We assume that states absorb one-half of the $7.6 billion reduction in federal spending ($3.8 
billion) through their general funds and the remaining half by reducing their Medicaid budgets. If one-
third of the Medicaid reduction ($1.3 billion) is accomplished through reductions in coverage, we 
estimate that 110,000 people would lose coverage in 2034. 
Immigrant Eligibility 
Beginning October 1, 2026, OBBBA 
allows federal Medicaid payments to states 
for services provided only to individuals who 
are either U.S. citizens or nationals, lawful 
permanent residents (LPRs) (i.e., green-card 
holders), Cuban or Haitian entrants, or 
citizens of the Federated States of 
Micronesia, the Marshall Islands, or Palau 
who lawfully reside in the United States 
under a Compact of Free Association (Pub. 
L. 119-21, 2025). States will continue to

receive federal matching funds for Medicaid coverage only for these groups and for some emergency 
services. 
All other noncitizen categories that are eligible for federally funded Medicaid will lose that 
eligibility—such as refugees, asylees, Iraqi and Afghan special immigrant visa holders, Amerasians, 
certain humanitarian parolees, victims of human trafficking and their families, and individuals 
protected under the Violence Against Women Act. These groups will no longer qualify for any federal 
Medicaid reimbursement, though states can still choose to cover them using state-only funds. 
We project that in 2034, 120,000 fewer individuals will be enrolled in Medicaid. The reduced 
enrollment will lower total Medicaid spending by approximately $11 billion by 2034, yielding savings 
of about $4 billion for state general funds and $6.9 billion for the federal government.  
The vast majority of individuals affected by this legislation are refugees and asylees. However, the 
Trump administration has lowered the annual refugee admissions ceiling from 125,000 to 7,500, 
resulting in a relatively small number of newly arriving refugees. Consequently, most of the projected 
federal savings from this policy are expected to stem from the elimination of Medicaid access for the 
asylee population rather than for refugees because the ratio of refugees to asylees is anticipated to be 
approximately one to ten by 2034. 
The overall savings are not evenly distributed across states, reflecting the highly uneven geographic 
distribution of refugees and asylees. Our estimates indicate that approximately 50 percent of total 
state savings will be concentrated in just four states: California ($937 million), Texas ($431 million), 
Virginia ($363 million), and New York ($287 million). 
Cost-Sharing 
Beginning October 1, 2028, Medicaid 
expansion enrollees with family incomes 
between 100 and 138 percent of FPL could be 
subject to deductions, cost-sharing, or similar 
charges for certain medical services, subject to 
statutory limits that leave substantial 
discretion to states in determining the details 
of implementation (Pub. L. 119-21, 2025). 
For the purposes of this analysis, we assumed 
that cost-sharing is implemented in the form 
of copayments. Individual service charges 
cannot exceed $35, and total family cost-
sharing is capped at 5 percent of family income. Certain services—e.g., primary care, mental health 
care, and treatment for substance use disorders—remain exempt from cost-sharing. Within these 
parameters, states retain discretion to determine the specific amounts of cost-sharing. Given the 
relatively low levels of cost-sharing, we assumed that this provision would not prompt Medicaid 
enrollees to disenroll. We further assumed that services that are used more frequently would be 
subject to lower cost-sharing amounts, with copayments set at $10 for office visits, $20 for outpatient 
visits, and $35 for hospital stays. After accounting for the reduction in use associated with higher cost-

25 
sharing, we estimated that copayment revenue would reduce the overall state Medicaid budget by $1.7 
billion over the 2028–2034 period. Because the FMAP for the expansion population is 90 percent, the 
effect on state general funds is modest, approximately $0.2 billion, while the majority of the savings—
$1.5 billion—accrues to the federal budget. Although hospital stays carry the highest copayment, the 
majority of total savings would result from office visit copayments, which occur far more frequently 
than hospitalizations.  
This analysis is subject to substantial uncertainty, primarily because the level of cost-sharing that 
states will impose is unknown and difficult to forecast, and the copayment levels chosen in this analysis 
are purely indicative. The resulting estimates scale linearly with the assumed copayment amounts; for 
example, if all copayments were set at $35, total savings would be nearly three times larger. Moreover, 
it is uncertain whether the projected reductions of use will occur. Because Medicaid providers are 
generally required to furnish care even when copayments are not collected, low-income beneficiaries 
might not ultimately bear the intended cost-sharing burden. In that case, the policy could function 
more as a reduction in provider payments than as an effective cost-sharing mechanism. 
Despite these limitations, sensitivity analyses indicate that the central conclusion that the overall 
fiscal impact of this provision is small remains robust. 
Home- and Community-Based Services 
HCBS can be a cost-effective alternative 
to institutionalized care for people who need 
assistance with daily living. Although 
HCBS are not covered as a standard 
Medicaid benefit, all states offer HCBS in 
Medicaid through a waiver. However, 
previous 1915(c) waivers (the most common 
type of HCBS waiver) limited services to 
Medicaid enrollees who require an 
institutional level of care (CMS, undated-c). 
OBBBA broadens states’ ability to offer 
1915(c) HCBS waivers to enrollees who do 
not meet the criteria necessary to be eligible for institutionalized care (Pub. L. 119-21, 2025). 
Given that all states offer HCBS waivers of some type, we assume that the new HCBS waiver 
option will be popular. However, because many states will experience Medicaid funding cuts as a 
result of OBBBA, they might be unable to expand coverage to new HCBS populations. We therefore 
assume that only states that experience net gains to their general funds stemming from OBBBA by 
2034 (because of such provisions as work requirements, which effectively force states to reduce 
Medicaid spending) will apply for new HCBS waivers. We allocate CBO’s estimate of $6.6 billion in 
new HCBS funding across states with OBBBA-related general fund increases in proportion to 
Medicaid spending in the state. We estimate that the states with the largest new HCBS waiver 
spending (federal and state combined) between 2025 and 2034 will be Texas ($1.4 billion),

Rural Health Transformation Program 
The RHTP is designed to distribute $10 
billion annually between 2026 and 2030 (Pub. 
L. 119-21, 2025). The formula used to 
allocate funding has 50-percent weight on an 
even split among all states with a successful 
application and 50-percent weight on a 
percentile-based scoring system, which means 
that there is relatively little variation in the 
absolute amount of funding each state can 
receive. All states will receive between $100 
million and $300 million annually regardless 
of their size, rurality, or application quality. 
This is a relatively modest amount of money to a large state, such as California, but a significant 
windfall for a less populous state, such as Wyoming.  
We find that Kentucky will receive the largest annual funding allocation to their Medicaid budget, 
at $226 million; Rhode Island will receive the least, at $156 million. To contextualize these amounts 
and make comparisons across states, we divided estimated annual RHTP funding by each state’s 
projected total Medicaid spending in 2026, as forecast by the 2023 NHEA projections (CMS, 
2025d). The resulting percentages for each state are shown in Figure 3.3.

In percentage terms, Wyoming stands to gain the most from the RHTP, with program funds 
representing 25 percent of its 2026 Medicaid budget. California benefits the least; RHTP funding 
accounts for less than 0.2 percent of its expected Medicaid spending. Because state population varies 
more than funding amounts, population is the primary factor determining relative benefit, not rurality. 
Rhode Island, the least urban state under CMS’s methodology, stands to gain proportionally ten times 
more (4.6 percent of Medicaid spending) than Texas (0.4 percent), despite Texas being one of the 
most rural states. 
How states will spend these funds remains uncertain. CMS defines permissible uses for funds, 
with a focus on improving long-term care capacity in rural areas. Although RHTP funds cannot be 
used to draw down federal matching dollars, up to 15 percent can be used to increase provider 
payment rates, which could partially offset payment reductions that OBBBA imposed on SDPs.

28 
Chapter 4 
Discussion 
We estimated the impact of several key Medicaid provisions in OBBBA on states across 2025–
2034. We found that most state Medicaid programs will see substantial budget reductions because of 
these provisions, although the impact is somewhat reduced when considering the savings to state 
general funds in most states. There are several ways to categorize states when making generalizations 
about the expected impacts of these provisions: 
• Expansion states versus non-expansion states: States that have not expanded Medicaid are 
shielded from many of the Medicaid-related provisions in OBBBA, such as work 
requirements, more-frequent redeterminations, and cost-sharing. 
• Reliance on provider taxes and SDPs: These federal funding provisions will have a large 
impact on state budgets, but states that have no or minimal use of provider taxes or SDPs will 
see limited impacts of the provisions we studied. 
• Size of population: All states stand to gain federal funding from the RHTP, but states with 
smaller Medicaid populations and lower Medicaid spending gain more as a proportion of the 
total Medicaid budget. 
• Size of immigrant population: States with larger immigrant populations are likely to see 
larger impacts of the immigrant eligibility and emergency FMAP provisions. 
Comparisons with the Congressional Budget Office 
Our findings are broadly consistent with CBO (2025c) estimates of the federal funding impacts of 
these provisions. Table 4.1 shows comparisons of our findings with those of CBO. In general, our 
overall and provision-level estimates are fairly consistent with those produced by CBO. A few 
exceptions are as follows: 
• Address verification: Our estimate is about one-half the size of CBO’s. Although CBO’s 
exact methodologies and assumptions are not published, it is possible that CBO assumed that 
the simultaneous enrollments reported by the Office of the Inspector General (OIG) do not 
double-count (i.e., that individuals would appear only once in the table rather than once per 
state of enrollment). Our reading of the OIG report suggested that individuals would appear 
twice in the table, so we halved the number to get to incorrect enrollment. However, if CBO 
made the opposite assumption, it would explain the difference in results. 
• Cost-sharing: Our estimate is about 20 percent of CBO’s. The OBBBA provision on cost-
sharing requires that cost-sharing of greater than $0 and up to $35 be implemented for 
expansion adults with incomes at 100–138 percent of FPL. Therefore, there could be 
substantial variation in how the copays are applied. We assumed that copays would be $10 for

29 
office visits, $20 for outpatient visits, and $35 for hospital stays. However, if we assumed that 
all copays equaled $35, our estimate would roughly triple, putting us closer to CBO’s estimate. 
We also might have made different assumptions related to enrollees’ elasticity of demand (the 
expected change in demand for health care in response to a change in price).
