CBO also estimates the change in insurance, which might be lower than the change in Medicaid enrollment if people 
who disenroll from Medicaid find other sources of coverage. 
 
Another source of differences in RAND’s and CBO’s estimates is that CBO takes into account 
certain changes in state behavior (such as considering the impacts of provider taxes that are never 
enacted or SDPs in states that do not have them), although RAND does not. Other factors that 
might contribute to this discrepancy are differences in the inflation assumptions used for the 
projections—specifically, RAND’s use of NHEA projections—and differing assumptions about the 
timing and phase-in of certain programs. We also note that, although RAND reports changes in 
Medicaid enrollment, CBO reports changes in insurance coverage. We would expect insurance

30 
changes to be smaller than Medicaid enrollment changes because people who disenroll from Medicaid 
could find other sources of coverage. 
KFF estimates that $89 billion of the $226 billion of federal savings estimated by CBO comes 
from taxes (Euhus et al., 2025). If we net out this $89 billion, our estimates for the provider tax effects 
are notably higher than CBO’s estimate. Our enrollment effects for provider taxes are also somewhat 
higher than CBO’s. These dissimilarities could reflect differences in assumptions related to the FMAP 
rate at which Medicaid spending reductions are reimbursed, assumptions regarding whether some 
states might retain taxes above the hold harmless threshold (which is permissible in certain 
circumstances [Mitchell, 2024]), differences in data sources used to estimate the tax base, or other 
factors.  
Limitations 
We note several limitations of these analyses that should be considered when interpreting the 
findings. First, we did not model administrative costs associated with the provisions we studied. Many 
of the provisions have federal funding set aside for implementation, but we did not estimate the 
expected costs of implementing these provisions to the states or federal government or compare them 
with the allocated funding. Second, unlike CBO (2025c), we did not model future state policy 
changes—such as expanding Medicaid in the future or provider taxes that might have been 
implemented—because they would depend entirely on our ability to predict future state policy 
actions. Third, there are several related outcomes that we did not model, such as access to care (which 
could be affected by reduced SDPs) and uncompensated care (which could increase if OBBBA 
provisions lead to increased uninsurance). Access to care in particular could affect our findings to the 
extent that it could reduce use of care and therefore spending. Third, in our analysis of SDPs, we 
relied on several assumptions to assign dollar values to spending categories, including reliance on a 
paper that estimates Medicaid MCO spending by category of service (Powers, Ramani, and Meyer, 
2025). Although state-specific estimates (or estimates from more than one source) would have been 
preferable, data on Medicaid MCO spending by category were limited. Fourth, in our analysis of the 
RHTP, we assumed that all states receive grants. If some states fail to apply or CMS rejects certain 
applications, successful states will receive larger allocations that might be better targeted at rural states. 
Furthermore, we might understate funding variation between states because we assigned all states the 
median score for the 25 percent of scoring weight determined by policy adoption and proposal quality. 
However, even if the largest or most-rural states receive the highest scores in these categories, our core 
finding remains unchanged: Less populous states benefit most from the RHTP. Last, we relied on 
several assumptions to produce estimates of the impacts of these policies in future years, such as 
(1) that past years of data (for example, on SDP amounts, redetermination outcomes, and enrollment 
in Medicaid in more than one state) are representative of future years in the absence of OBBBA; 
(2) consistent with CBO (2025c), that states will follow a 50-50 split between Medicaid funding and 
general funding when states need to reduce spending; and (3) that Medicaid enrollment and spending 
will continue to grow along the lines of NHEA projections (CMS, 2025d). Using these projections 
allowed us to apply standardized growth rates across states and therefore allows for better cross-state 
comparisons but does not capture the nuance that state-specific estimates would. Although we believe

31 
that these assumptions are reasonable, uncertainty is inherent to work projecting future outcomes, and 
it is possible that actual behavior would vary from our assumptions. 
Policy Implications 
Changes to state Medicaid budgets as a result of the OBBBA provisions we studied vary from 
reductions of 19 percent to gains of 10 percent, and 26 states could be facing losses of 5 percent or 
more of their Medicaid budgets. Some of these policies effectively require states to reduce the sizes of 
their Medicaid programs, either via lower enrollment (e.g., work requirements, more-frequent 
redetermination) or lower payment rates (e.g., SDPs) (Pub. L. 119-21, 2025). These policies reduce 
Medicaid spending by both the state and federal governments. However, the provisions related to 
provider taxes, emergency FMAP, and the good faith waiver for erroneous payments essentially 
reduce federal funding without reducing the size of the Medicaid program, requiring states to either 
find other funding sources to bridge the gap or reduce Medicaid spending by reducing eligibility, 
covered services, provider payment rates, or some combination of those. Depending on the size of 
these relative effects, states could see increases or decreases to their state general funds; the impact 
ranged from −6 percent to 5 percent, with 31 states seeing increases to general funds. We also 
estimated enrollment impacts and found that Medicaid enrollment will fall by 7.6 million in 2034. 
More broadly, states might also wish to consider measures to address the changes brought about 
by the OBBBA provisions that we studied. For example, the SDP provision will serve to reduce 
directed payments to some provider types. If the reductions lead to fewer providers accepting 
Medicaid—and therefore decreased access to care for Medicaid enrollees—states could consider other 
actions that they could take to improve access. There could also be increased uncompensated care if 
uninsurance rates rise or greater strain on emergency departments and local health clinics. States will 
also need to determine how to allocate funds from the RHTP, which might offset access-to-care issues 
in rural areas. 
States wishing to document the observed impacts of OBBBA Medicaid provisions over time might 
consider tracking (1) Medicaid enrollment, both overall and specifically among the expansion 
population; (2) the rate of Medicaid disenrollments because of procedural reasons; (3) uninsurance 
rates; (4) the proportion of primary care and non–primary care providers that accept Medicaid; 
(5) use of HCBS in Medicaid; and (6) access to care in rural areas. 
Conclusions 
Over the next decade, most state Medicaid programs will experience substantial impacts as a result 
of OBBBA. The provisions will directly affect Medicaid financing, eligibility, generosity, and access to 
care; they might have broader implications as well. States will likely need to draw more heavily from 
state general funds, reduce enrollment, or reduce coverage to bridge the gaps, and they might need to 
consider other actions to address broader issues related to access and uninsurance.

32 
Appendix A 
Detailed Methodology 
We relied on NHEA data (CMS, 2025d) to project spending and enrollment to future years. 
This allows us to use a standardized growth rate, facilitating comparisons across states. In the 
following sections, we describe the detailed methodology for each of our provision-specific estimates 
and for the overall estimates. 
Overall Analysis 
General Methodology 
To estimate the overall effect of OBBBA, we used a two-step process. First, we defined how each 
provision changes each of four budgets. Second, we simulated how states might respond to these 
budget changes. This process allowed us to account for the interactions between policies. 
We assume that the provisions in OBBBA can be represented as transfers between four 
interconnected budgets: the federal government (F), the state general fund (G), Medicaid expenditures 
(M), and non-Medicaid expenditures (N); this last budget comes into play only for provider tax 
changes, which can reduce taxes levied on non-Medicare providers.  
The first-order effects of each provision are represented as budget flows: 
• Medicaid cuts, whether they involve reduced enrollment (such as work requirements) or rate 
decreases (such as SDPs), transfer money from Medicaid to both state general funds and the 
federal government. The proportion of the savings that go to the federal government depends 
on the FMAP. We assume cuts to ACA expansion populations use 90 percent FMAP; other 
cuts use each state’s average FMAP from the 2024 MBES financial reports. 
• Federal funding reductions (such as reductions in emergency FMAP funding) transfer 
money from state general funds to the federal government. For example, if federal FMAP 
drops from 90 percent to 50 percent on $100 million in emergency care, the federal 
government saves $40 million and the state loses $40 million—but the same amount of care 
still occurs, leaving the Medicaid budget unaffected.  
• General fund cuts (provider tax cuts) reduce state general fund revenue, with no direct flow 
of funding back to the federal government. However, we assume that states will respond to 
these tax cuts by lowering the Medicaid budget to offset the lost state revenue.  
To model how states respond to budget changes, we defined a budget goal and allowed states to 
cut their Medicaid spending to meet it. Following CBO, we assumed states would replace one-half of 
any lost federal funding through their general fund and generate the other half by cutting Medicaid 
spending (CBO, 2025b). This means that states make equal cuts to their Medicaid programs and

33 
general fund. Because Medicaid savings partially accrue to the federal government through reduced 
federal matching, states must make larger cuts than their target savings amount to balance their 
budgets. For instance, in a state with an FMAP of 0.5, generating $10 billion in state savings requires 
$13.3 billion in total cuts: about $6.6 billion from the general fund and $6.6 billion from Medicaid (of 
which the state retains only $3.3 billion, with the other $3.3 billion representing lost federal matching 
funds). This effect increases federal savings and becomes more pronounced as the FMAP increases. 
States have several options to cut Medicaid spending, such as lowering payment rates, limiting 
services, or restricting enrollment. In cases in which we model the enrollment impacts of budget 
reductions (i.e., for provider taxes, good faith waivers, and emergency FMAP), we assume that states 
would make up one-third of the reduction in their Medicaid budgets by reducing enrollment. We 
further assume that these reductions would affect all enrollees equally, which would be the case if they 
were operationalized through increased administrative barriers to enrolling. Because of this 
assumption, we estimate state savings based on the average FMAP. We do not allow states to cut 
funds from the RHTP because these funds are earmarked by CMS for specific capacity-building 
initiatives. 
Although states can decrease Medicaid spending in response to these changes, we assume that they 
cannot increase spending because OBBBA (Pub. L. 119-21, 2025) limits many of the tools that states 
have to increase Medicaid expenditures, such as SDPs. Other methods to increase Medicaid spending, 
such as increased base payment rates, are more difficult to use because states must abide by the Upper 
Payment Limit and might be required to submit State Plan Amendments (SPAs) or waivers for CMS 
approval (Medicaid and CHIP Payment and Access Commission, 2021). The exception to this is the 
new HCBS waivers introduced in OBBBA. We assume that only states with a general fund surplus 
would implement these waivers, because they are partially funded by the state. We split the CBO 
(2025c) estimate for federal spending on HCBS waivers between states with a general fund budget 
surplus in proportion to the size of their Medicaid program.  
Our assumptions about how states will respond to funding cuts are very uncertain. In reality, state 
responses will vary based on such factors as budget pressures, political decisions, and the needs of the 
population. We have followed CBO (2025c) in assuming that states would make up 50 percent of the 
funding shortfall by cutting the Medicaid budget and 50 percent by leveraging the general fund. If 
states were to address the entire funding shortfall by cutting Medicaid, the cuts would have to be 
substantial (twice as large as what we have estimated), because every dollar that the state saved would 
require forgoing at least a dollar of federal matching. Furthermore, states might have limited options 
to cut Medicaid spending because some services and eligibility groups are mandatory, and provider 
payment rates are already low (Medicaid and CHIP Payment and Access Commission, undated-b). 
Although cuts that target working-age adults might be more politically palatable than cuts to other 
groups (such as children and people with disabilities), the high federal matching rate (90 percent) for 
working-age adults means that reductions in spending on this group generate limited savings for 
states. Leveraging the general fund would avoid the need to cut Medicaid and would result in a lower 
total budget impact to the state because it would not require forgoing federal funding. However, it 
might be difficult or impossible for states to repurpose resources from the general fund or to levy new 
taxes.

34 
Table A.1 shows how sensitive the budgetary impacts can be to alternative assumptions about 
how states finance the cuts. For presentational purposes, we assume an FMAP of 0.67, the median 
average FMAP across states. If a state were to finance a $1,000 reduction in provider taxes or federal 
spending by making offsetting cuts to Medicaid, it would need to cut $3,030 in Medicaid spending, 
losing $2,030 in federal funding.7 With the assumption that the state addresses one-half of the 
funding reduction by leveraging the general fund, the state would need to cut $1,515 in Medicaid 
spending, losing $1,015 in federal funding. If the state financed the full spending reduction by 
leveraging the general fund, the Medicaid budget would be unaffected. These calculations indicate that 
our results, particularly for provider taxes, rely heavily on assumptions about how states will address 
the loss of tax funding. In particular, if we were to assume that Medicaid bore 100 percent of the tax 
cuts, the reduction in the Medicaid budget could be twice as high as what we have shown in the main 
body of this report. Conversely, if the state replaced the reduced tax revenue with other revenue from 
the general fund, provider tax cuts would have no impact on Medicaid funding.

Accounting for Policy Interactions and Overlap 
We accounted for interactions between policies, such as changes in the provider tax base and 
overlapping state responses to different provisions. For example, if a state faces tax revenue loss, it 
might reduce Medicaid spending. If that same state must also cut spending by reducing SDPs, we 
avoided double-counting these reductions. However, we made some simplifying assumptions. We 
assumed that first-order budget changes are independent and that provider tax spending reductions 
affect only the providers subject to the tax. For instance, we assumed that cuts to hospital provider tax 
revenue lead to reductions in hospital spending. 
This process generated estimates of the overall budgetary effect of OBBBA’s Medicaid provisions. 
To estimate the effect of individual provisions, we repeated this process for each provision in isolation, 
assuming that it was the only provision implemented. Because these individual estimates do not 
account for interactions between policies, they do not sum to our overall estimate. However, they are 
useful for understanding the relative importance of different provisions. 
 
7 If the state were offsetting a direct cut in federal Medicaid spending, this $2,030 loss in federal funding would be in addition to 
the initial cut. This is not the case with provider tax reductions, for which there is no direct cut in federal funding.

35 
Because states vary in population size, comparing absolute dollar values of budget changes across 
states can be misleading. To make comparisons across states, we standardized budget changes by 
dividing them by the state’s total Medicaid spending, estimated from the State Health Expenditure 
Accounts (CMS, 2024b). We refer to this as the percentage Medicaid budget change. We match the 
periods in the denominator and numerator, so if we use budget changes from 2025 to 2034 in the 
numerator, we sum NHEA Medicaid spending totals from 2025 to 2034 (CMS, 2025d) in the 
denominator. 
Estimating Enrollment Changes 
We also consider that policies that affect enrollment might target overlapping groups of 
individuals. For example, individuals who would lose coverage because of Medicaid work requirements 
might overlap with those who would lose coverage because of more-frequent redeterminations. For the 
three policies that affect enrollment directly (work requirements, redeterminations, and immigrant 
eligibility changes), we started with a population of individuals that could be affected by at least one of 
the eligibility provisions and applied the exclusions sequentially. We note that we do not consider 
reduced enrollments resulting from enrollee address verification to be true reductions in enrollment 
because those individuals would have coverage in another state; therefore, although those reductions 
contribute to our estimates of budgetary impacts, they do not contribute to our estimates of 
enrollment changes. 
We also estimated the enrollment effects of three additional policies that could have an indirect 
effect on enrollment because of reduced state budgets (provider taxes, reduction in emergency FMAP, 
and limitations on the good faith waiver). To estimate the enrollment loss from each of these indirect 
provisions, we assumed that states would address one-half of the federal funding reduction by 
reducing Medicaid spending and that one-third of the Medicaid reduction (one-sixth of the total 
reduction) would be accomplished through enrollment changes. To get the un-deduplicated 
enrollment impact for each of these three policies, we divided the one-sixth spending reduction target 
by the state-specific per capita cost of a Medicaid enrollee, estimated at the average FMAP.  
To deduplicate the indirect enrollment effects from the direct enrollment effects, we subtracted 
the savings from the direct policies from the one-sixth Medicaid reduction that states would need to 
accomplish for the indirect policies. We then calculated the number of people who would need to be 
disenrolled (at the average FMAP) to address the balance of the spending reduction. Finally, we 
allocated these enrollment cuts across the indirect provisions in proportion to the overall Medicaid 
budgetary impact of each of the three indirect enrollment policies. 
Work Requirements 
Because analyzing the populations affected by work requirements requires detailed information on 
income, employment, family structure, disability status, and other individual characteristics, we 
constructed state-specific micro-level datasets containing all relevant variables. These datasets were 
constructed primarily from the 2023 ACS (U.S. Census Bureau, 2025), the most-recent data available 
at the time of analysis. For the purposes of this study, we included Wisconsin because it is treated as

36 
an expansion state as a result of the application of work requirements. Unlike other expansion states, 
however, Wisconsin’s expansion adult group is limited to individuals with incomes of less than 100 
percent of the FPL rather than up to 133 percent, and the FMAP for this population is the state’s 
regular Medicaid matching rate rather than the enhanced expansion FMAP. 
A central step in constructing these data was identifying, among Medicaid enrollees ages 19–64, 
those who fall into the new adult group, also known as Group VIII, as referred to in the 
ACA/Medicaid enrollment. We made the following assumptions:  
• Pregnant women and women in the postpartum Medicaid period are not included in the new 
adult group because their income eligibility thresholds typically exceed 138 percent of the 
FPL. 
• Individuals receiving supplemental security income (SSI) or enrolled in Medicare are also 
excluded from the new adult group; most qualify for Medicaid on the basis of disability rather 
than through expansion. 
• Residents of institutional group quarters—such as jails, prisons, nursing homes, and 
psychiatric facilities—are not eligible for the new adult group, although individuals residing in 
noninstitutional group quarters might qualify. 
• Individuals with disabilities, as identified by at least one ACS disability indicator, might be 
included in the expansion group unless they are enrolled in SSI or Medicare. This reflects the 
very low income thresholds, substantial evidentiary requirements, and long processing times 
associated with disability-based Medicaid pathways, which together imply that only a small 
share of disabled adults in expansion states would enroll through the traditional blind or 
disabled category. 
• Most adults with dependent, insured children might also fall within the new adult group 
because parental eligibility thresholds under traditional Medicaid are generally low. However, 
in the nine states where parental eligibility exceeds 138 percent of the FPL, we classify all 
adults with dependent children as outside the new adult group. 
After applying these criteria to identify a population whose characteristics closely resemble those 
of the new adult group’s expansion population, we adjusted the sample to account for Medicaid 
unwinding and estimated the size of the new adult group in 2024. The resulting estimate, 19.7 million 
individuals, is compared with the official 2024 estimate of 20.4 million from CMS (2025e). Because 
the two figures differ by only 3.3 percent, we concluded that our selected sample provides a sufficiently 
accurate representation of the new adult group population. To ensure full alignment with 
administrative data, however, we reweight the ACS sample to match the official state-level new adult 
group enrollment counts exactly. 
To assign health care expenditures to individuals in the dataset, we relied on the KFF dataset 
“Medicaid Spending per Enrollee by Enrollment Group,” which is based on analysis of Medicaid 
Transformed Medicaid Statistical Information System (T-MSIS) files for 2023 and reports 
expenditures specifically for the expansion adult population (KFF, undated-b). Expenditures were 
then inflated to future years using NHEA Medicaid projections (CMS, 2025d).  
After constructing the micro-level dataset, we simulated the work requirements by applying all 
compliance and exemption criteria specified in the legislation to identify the set of individuals who

37 
would be deemed noncompliant. Two variables necessary for this step—school enrollment and 
participation in volunteer activities—are not available in the ACS. We therefore imputed these 
variables using data from the 2023 Current Population Survey (CPS) October Supplement on School 
Enrollment (U.S. Census Bureau, 2024a) and the CPS September 2023 Volunteering and Civic Life 
Supplement (U.S. Census Bureau, 2024b), respectively. The datasets are quite small and supported 
only a very simple imputation based on age, sex, employment status, and income. Including additional 
variables, such as household structure, left the imputation unaltered.  
OBBBA does not specify how states should determine disability status. In the ACS (U.S. Census 
Bureau, 2025), disability is measured through six questions, and respondents who answer “yes” to any 
one item are classified as having a disability. This definition aligns with national disability rates, but 
for our purposes, it appeared too broad and prone to substantial measurement error. In addition, 
because it sits at the extreme of the scale, it provides little scope for sensitivity analysis. We therefore 
classified individuals as disabled only if they answered “yes” to at least two of the six ACS items. The 
likelihood of misclassification is substantially lower when based on two affirmative responses rather 
than one, making this approach more robust. 
After identifying individuals who fail to meet the community-engagement requirement—
predominantly, those not participating in the labor force—we assigned their post-disenrollment 
insurance status using the following assumptions: 
• Individuals who already have access to alternative coverage are assumed to leave Medicaid for 
that coverage.  
• Those with a spouse who holds employer-sponsored insurance are assumed to disenroll and 
join the spouse’s plan.  
• Individuals who fall short of the hourly work requirement are assumed to increase their work 
hours sufficiently to maintain Medicaid eligibility. This assumption might be somewhat 
conservative and might underestimate the number of individuals losing Medicaid coverage. 
However, the number of individuals falling in this category is very small.  
• All remaining noncompliant individuals are assumed to lose Medicaid coverage and become 
uninsured. 
Individuals who fail to meet the work requirements are not the only ones expected to lose 
Medicaid coverage. Evidence from states that implemented Medicaid work requirements, particularly 
Arkansas, indicates that many coverage losses occurred among individuals who were actually 
compliant or exempt. For these individuals, disenrollment was driven by administrative and 
informational barriers—such as lack of awareness, confusion about reporting rules, or difficulties 
completing paperwork—rather than failure to meet work requirements. Other studies in the literature 
have used data from Arkansas to estimate the magnitude of this effect (Harker, 2023; Karpman, 
Haley, and Kenney, 2025; Sommers et al., 2020). However, we decided to use a different approach, 
for the following reasons:  
• The Arkansas program was implemented for a relatively short period, limiting the time 
available for beneficiaries to become aware of the requirements and for the state Medicaid 
agency to fully develop and operationalize the necessary administrative systems. In contrast, 
the legislation analyzed here mandates a phase-in period extending through 2029, providing

38 
states with substantially more time to implement the program and allowing beneficiaries 
greater opportunity to learn about and adapt to the new requirements. 
• The affected population was small and limited to individuals ages 30–49, which constrains the 
available empirical evidence. As a result, it is unclear whether it is appropriate to base a 
nationwide analysis primarily on such a narrow and limited set of findings. 
• We expect substantial heterogeneity across states in the efficiency and accuracy with which 
modifications to the Medicaid program are implemented; accordingly, it is important to 
account for state-level variation in the analysis. 
Accordingly, we relied on CMS’s “State Medicaid and CHIP Eligibility Processing Data” dataset 
(2025k), which allows us to estimate, for each state, the share S of enrollees who lose Medicaid 
coverage for procedural or administrative reasons. This dataset is well suited to capturing the 
administrative barriers that can lead to coverage loss—often temporarily—and it exhibits substantial 
variation across states. By applying the estimated 𝑆 to the number of Medicaid enrollees in our model 
who are compliant with or exempt from work requirements, we estimated the number of individuals 
who lose Medicaid coverage despite meeting eligibility or exemption criteria.  
Once we had computed the total number of individuals disenrolling from Medicaid, we estimated 
the savings to the federal and state government by applying the FMAP to their health care 
expenditures.  
Implementing work requirements requires states to establish administrative systems capable of 
verifying compliance accurately and efficiently. The provision takes effect in 2027, and the legislation 
mandates that full implementation must be completed by 2029. Following the approach of CBO 
(2025c), we assume a phased rollout between 2027 and 2029, with the program reaching full 
operational capacity by the beginning of 2030. 
Provider Taxes 
States can use revenues from taxes on health care providers to finance their share of Medicaid 
spending. When combined with other policies that increase Medicaid spending, such as rate increases 
from SDPs or eligibility expansions, provider taxes enable states to draw down additional federal 
funding. For example, a state might add a provider tax and simultaneously increase Medicaid payment. 
The state could then draw FMAP on the increased provider payment. Medicaid providers would be 
no worse off if their payment increased in a manner that offset the tax. But the state retains both the 
tax revenue and the additional FMAP. Provider taxes are widely used; we found that, as of OBBBA’s 
passage, 45 states and the District of Columbia had active or recently enacted provider taxes. 
The federal government has long recognized the potential for abuse of provider taxes (defined as 
taxes for which at least 85 percent of revenue comes from health care providers [Social Security 
Administration, undated]) and has imposed restrictions on their implementation. In theory, these 
taxes must be “broad-based” and “uniform,” meaning that they are levied on all providers within a class 
(such as hospitals) at the same rate (Social Security Administration, undated). Additionally, providers 
cannot be “held harmless”—that is, guaranteed to receive their tax payments back through other 
means (Social Security Administration, undated).

39 
In practice, many states have circumvented these requirements. Using our review of state policies,8 
all states have avoided the “held harmless” test by keeping their taxes below a 6-percent threshold. In 
addition, states can obtain waivers from CMS exempting them from the broad-based and uniform tax 
requirements by demonstrating that tax revenues and Medicaid payments are not positively correlated 
(known as the generally redistributive test). States discovered that they could pass the generally 
redistributive test while still disproportionately taxing Medicaid providers by levying higher taxes on 
smaller providers—a practice that CMS criticized in waiver letters but could not prevent (CMS, 
undated-d). 
The provider tax provisions of OBBBA are designed to reduce the size and prevalence of provider 
taxes and close loopholes in the uniformity test, thereby limiting the amount of federal Medicaid 
dollars that states can draw down. These provisions have three main components. First, a moratorium 
on new taxes is enforced by setting the hold harmless threshold at 0 percent for any new taxes. Second, 
the hold harmless threshold for taxes will be gradually reduced from 6 percent in 2027 to 3.5 percent 
in 2032. This lower threshold does not apply to states that chose not to expand Medicaid under the 
ACA or to taxes on nursing facilities and ICFs. Finally, OBBBA gives CMS greater discretion to 
determine whether a tax is uniformly imposed, allowing the agency to deny waivers when a tax falls 
disproportionately on Medicaid providers. 
Identifying Provider Taxes 
To model changes to provider taxes, we constructed a database of all affected taxes and applied the 
new threshold and waiver rules to each. We created our tax database using the categorization from the 
2024 KFF survey of Medicaid directors, which breaks provider taxes into classes: MCOs, hospitals, 
ambulance, ICFs, nursing facilities, and other (Burns et al., 2025). We excluded ICFs and nursing 
facilities from our review because these provider classes are exempt from the decrease in hold harmless 
thresholds and are unlikely to be affected by the more stringent waiver conditions. We reviewed each 
tax listed under “other” and determined that they had been repealed, had tax rates below 3.5 percent, 
or had tax bases small enough to be negligible in the context of other provider taxes. We then 
conducted our own search for MCO, hospital, and ambulance provider taxes in each state and 
reviewed state laws to identify the implementation details for each tax. 
We collected four data points for each state: Medicaid and non-Medicaid tax bases (i.e., taxable 
revenues in each program) and Medicaid and non-Medicaid tax rates in percentage terms. These data 
points fully define a tax because they allow for the calculation of tax revenues in each program and the 
overall tax rate. Following the definition in the law, we used net patient revenue for each tax base 
(Code of Federal Regulations, Title 42, Part 433).  
Managed Care Taxes 
Taxes on MCOs are generally structured either as a percentage of net patient revenues or with a 
complex per-member-per-month structure designed to meet the generally redistributive test while 
 
8 Our review involved analysis of data retrieved between May and November 2025 from state codes and statutes, health 
departments, departments of insurance, and HCRIS.

40 
disproportionately taxing Medicaid providers. When taxes were based on a percentage of revenues, we 
used data on net patient revenues from the 2023 National Association of Insurance Commissioners 
Annual Report to estimate the tax base. We then applied tax rates that we acquired through our 
search of state laws to get tax revenue amounts. We calculated non-Medicaid revenues as the sum of 
individual comprehensive and group comprehensive plans because Medicare Advantage and federal 
employee plans cannot be taxed by states, and most taxes exclude limited plans (such as dental or 
vision). When taxes were levied only on health maintenance organizations (HMOs), we reduced the 
size of the tax base to align it with the enrollment share of HMOs in that region of the country (Chan 
et al., 2023; KFF, 2024). We assumed that all Medicaid managed care plans were HMOs. 
When taxes had a more complex structure, we manually identified the required data and applied 
the tax rules. For instance, California’s MCO tax applies only to plan member-months between 1.25 
and 4 million. In 2025, Medicaid plans are taxed at $274 per member-month while non-Medicaid 
plans are taxed at $1.75 per member-month (California Department of Health Care Services, 2025). 
To identify the number of member-months and total revenues for each plan, we used the 2024 annual 
reports from the California Department of Managed Health Care (California Department of 
Managed Health Care, undated).We then applied these rates to calculate total Medicaid and non-
Medicaid revenues and converted them to percentages so that we could model how the tax would be 
affected by the reduction in the hold harmless threshold. We used similar manual processes for 
Illinois (Illinois Department of Insurance, 2025a and 2025b), Massachusetts (Center for Health 
Information and Analysis, Commonwealth of Massachusetts, undated), Michigan (Michigan 
Department of Insurance and Financial Services, undated), New York (New York State Department 
of Health, 2025a and 2025b), Ohio (Ohio Department of Medicaid, 2025), Pennsylvania 
(Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, undated), and West Virginia (West Virginia Department of 
Health, Health Facilities, and Human Services, 2024). 
Hospital Provider Taxes 
Hospital provider taxes followed a similar pattern. Many states apply taxes as a percentage of net 
patient revenues; others have complex taxes with differential rates depending on inpatient versus 
outpatient revenues, hospital type, or per diem charges. For all states except California, we collected 
data from HCRIS (CMS, 2025h) to generate the tax base and applied the relevant tax rules derived 
based on our search of state laws. For California, we used data from the state’s calendar year 2024 
Hospital Quality Assurance Program fee model (California Department of Health Care Services, 
undated). When taxes used a base other than net patient revenues (for instance, gross revenue), we 
recalculated the percentage based on net patient revenues. If states published estimates of their total 
hospital tax revenues, we verified that our estimates were consistent with these totals. 
Ambulance Provider Taxes 
Taxes on ambulances or emergency transports were generally structured as either a per-transport 
rate or a percentage of net revenues from transports. It was more difficult to find data on ambulance 
revenues than for MCOs or hospitals. We used revenue information from state-level ambulance 
industry reports and transport counts from state financial reports (IBISWorld, 2025) to estimate the 
tax base. To split revenues between Medicaid and non-Medicaid in all states, we applied the national

41 
ratio of Medicaid to non-Medicaid spending observed in analysis of the Ground Ambulance Data 
Collection System (Mulcahy et al., 2024). Although we acknowledge the lower quality of the data 
used for ambulance taxes, the magnitude of these taxes is very small (less than 1 percent of hospital 
and MCO taxes), so our overall results are insensitive even to large errors in this provider tax class. 
We then estimated tax revenues by applying the tax rates we identified in our review of state laws. 
Modeling Provider Taxes 
To model the impact of provider taxes, we calculated what the Medicaid and non-Medicaid tax 
rates would be for each state in each year. We assumed that taxes in non-expansion states or for 
exempted provider classes were unaffected. We also assumed that other states would follow the hold 
harmless threshold as it is reduced. For example, a tax that is 6 percent in 2025 would be reduced to 
5.5 percent in 2026, 5 percent in 2027, and so on. 
When CMS originally proposed closing the generally redistributive loophole, they identified eight 
taxes in seven states that would no longer receive waivers under this proposed rule. CMS waiver letters 
indicate that these taxes include the hospital tax in California and MCO taxes in California, Michigan, 
Massachusetts, and New York (CMS, 2025b). We assume that the three remaining taxes are the 
MCO taxes in Illinois, Ohio, and West Virginia, based on their non-uniform design and on analysis 
by researchers at Georgetown (Park and Corlette, 2025). When a state loses a waiver, we assume that 
it keeps its tax in place but reduces its Medicaid tax rate (which is the higher rate for these taxes) to 
the non-Medicaid rate. This allows states to maximize their remaining income, comply with the new 
uniformity requirements, and avoid imposing additional taxes on non-Medicaid providers (CMS, 
2025c). 
We included all taxes enacted as of the passage of OBBBA that began collection by January 2026, 
but we did not attempt to model the effect of the moratorium in future years. This would have 
required estimating, under a counterfactual scenario, which states would increase their tax rates or 
establish new provider taxes from 2026 to 2034; such an estimate is subject to significant uncertainty 
and is beyond the scope of this analysis. This means that our estimates are likely to be conservative 
because the trend has been the expansion of provider taxes both within and across states (Burns et al., 
2025). 
Each year, the revenue loss to the state is equal to the tax base multiplied by the change in the 
overall provider tax rate. On its own, the reduction in the tax rate does not change federal spending. 
However, in the earlier “Overall Analysis” section in this appendix, we describe how this generates 
savings to the federal government by inducing the state to make cuts to Medicaid to cover the budget 
deficit. 
State-Directed Payments 
Sec. 71116 of OBBBA sets the maximum SDP for inpatient and outpatient services, professional 
services at an AMC, and nursing facility services at 100 percent of the total published Medicare rate 
for Medicaid expansion states and at 110 percent of the total published Medicare rate for non-
expansion states (Pub. L. 119-21, 2025). This essentially repeals a Biden-era rule that allows SDPs to

42 
be set as high as the average commercial payment rate. This provision applies immediately to new 
SDP applications but phases in starting in 2028 for renewals of SDPs in 10–percentage point 
reductions per year. 
We estimated the likely impacts of the reduction to the SDP limit by reviewing all SDP approval 
packages for SDPs approved as of July 19, 2025, with end dates in 2025 (CMS, undated-a). The 
approval packages follow a prespecified format, so we used an automated algorithm to extract the 
following data elements from each package: 
• Item 1: contract rating period (start and end date of the SDP) 
• Item 4: total dollar amount of the SDP 
- Item 4a: federal share of the SDP 
- Item 4b: non-federal share of the SDP 
• Item 6: whether the SDP is a new application, renewal application, or amendment application 
• Item 20: provider class(es) affected by the SDP 
• Table 2: summary of the SDP amount for each affected provider class 
• Item 24: indication of how the SDP percentages in Table 2 are defined (e.g., percentage of 
Medicare payment or cost, percentage of ACR, etc.). 
We then reviewed the set of SDPs with end dates in 2025 and excluded those that did not contain 
at least one of the provider classes affected by the OBBBA SDP provision (inpatient, outpatient, 
professional services at an AMC, or nursing facility). We then determined the number of SDP items 
in each SDP (i.e., the number of provider classes or subclasses affected by the SDP). Provider classes 
were selected from a prespecified list in the form and fell into the following categories: inpatient 
hospital, outpatient hospital, professional services at an AMC, primary care, specialty care, nursing 
facility, behavioral health inpatient, behavioral health outpatient, dental, and other. Subclasses, on the 
other hand, were entered into an open-ended field and therefore varied from SDP to SDP but 
included, for example, various classes of inpatient hospitals or specific specialty providers. 
After narrowing the set of SDPs to those containing at least one of the affected provider classes 
and with an end date in 2025,9 we assigned dollar values and provider classes to each SDP line because 
these were provided only at the SDP level (CMS, undated-a). To do so, we took the following steps: 
1. We consulted Table 2 in each approval package to determine how many provider classes or 
subclasses would be affected by the policy change. 
2. In some cases, if Table 2 was missing information, we relied instead on Item 20, which 
indicates the provider classes affected by the SDP. Without supporting information from 
Table 2, we assumed that there was a single SDP item per provider class. 
3. If Table 2 did not indicate the total payment amount but Item 24 indicated that the payment 
was defined as a percentage of ACR, we assumed that the total payment was set at 100 percent 
of ACR. 
4. For packages that consist of a single provider class and item, the total value of the package was 
assigned. 
 
9 We included one Texas SDP that has an end date in 2027 but clearly indicates the dollar value of the SDP for each year.

43 
5. For packages with a single provider class but multiple items (generally subclasses), the total 
value of the package was divided equally among the items. 
6. For packages with multiple provider classes, a member of the research team assigned each item 
to a provider class when possible based on the text entered into the provider class field. 
7. We relied on published estimates of Medicaid MCO spending by category of service (Powers, 
Ramani, and Meyer, 2025). The categories in those estimates were dental, skilled nursing 
facility (SNF) or HCBS, inpatient, outpatient, professional, pharmacy, and other. We aligned 
these with the categories in the SDP approval packages per Box A.1. 
Then, we allocated the total dollar amount of the SDP (from Item 4) proportionally to 
the spending by category of care from the published estimates. 
 
8. In some cases, the SDP approval packages published online did not directly provide usable 
information and instead referred to attachments that were not available online. In these cases, 
we reached out to states to request the attachments. Most states responded with the requested 
information, but one state was excluded from the analysis because of lack of information in the 
SDP approval package. 
We assumed that 2025 was a representative year for SDPs in terms of dollar value; distribution 
across states; distribution across provider classes; mix of new, renewal, and amendment SDPs; and 
federal versus state share. We relied on NHEA estimates (CMS, 2025d) to inflate the dollar value of 
the SDP amounts to future years. Because new SDPs are subject to the full impact of the OBBBA 
SDP provision right away, whereas grandfathered renewal SDPs are phased in, we made assumptions 
about the proportion of SDPs that would be grandfathered in each year. To do so, we made the 
assumption that the percentage P of new packages in 2025 was representative of the portion of new 
packages in future years, so for each year, we randomly designated an additional P × (number of SDPs) 
of the approval packages as new rather than renewal. This allowed us to account for the fact that the 
number of grandfathered renewal packages would decrease. This matters because renewal packages are 
required to reduce the SDP amount incrementally until they reach the newly allowed amount, 
whereas new SDPs must meet the requirement immediately.

44 
More-Frequent Redetermination 
OBBBA requires that state Medicaid programs redetermine Medicaid eligibility for the expansion 
population every six months rather than every 12. At the time of redetermination, enrollees could lose 
coverage for one of two reasons: (1) because of changes in circumstances that result in the individual 
becoming ineligible (e.g., changes in income, pregnancy status, or age) or (2) for procedural or 
administrative reasons (e.g., failure to provide the required documentation or failure to respond to 
state requests for follow-up information). 
We obtained Medicaid renewal data from August 2024 through September 2025 from CMS 
(2025a), which provides state-level information on the number of Medicaid renewals each month, the 
number whose coverage was renewed, and the number whose coverage was discontinued because of 
eligibility changes or administrative reasons. We did not use data from prior to August 2024 to avoid 
overlap with the broader reinstatement of Medicaid renewals following the end of the coronavirus 
disease 2019 (COVID-19) public health emergency. 
We combined these data with Medicaid enrollment data at the state level to determine what 
percentage of each state’s Medicaid expansion population was likely to be up for renewal each month. 
We then considered how more-frequent redetermination would affect enrollment. For those 
individuals for whom changes in circumstances lead to eligibility loss at renewal, we assumed that the 
month in which their circumstances change is evenly distributed. For one-half of these people, more-
frequent redetermination would have no impact because their eligibility change would happen in the 
latter half of the 12-month period. For the other half, their Medicaid coverage would decrease by one 
to six months, so we calculated that, on average, one-half of this population would lose 3.5 months of 
Medicaid coverage.10 
For individuals who might lose coverage because of administrative reasons, we assume that the 
number would increase by 50 percent because of more-frequent redetermination. Because of a lack of 
data or literature to inform this estimate, we made this assumption using the logic that, in theory, 
coverage loss because of administrative reasons could double if it happened randomly. However, it 
seems likely that there is some bias in that those who lose coverage at the first renewal are likely to be 
those who face greater challenges to providing the necessary documentation. We also accounted for 
the fact that those who lose coverage could re-enroll in less than a year, relying on estimates published 
by KFF (Corallo et al., 2021). These estimates suggest that, among expansion adults who disenrolled, 
4.1 percent re-enrolled within three months, 7.2 percent within six months, 10.1 percent within nine 
months, and 11.8 percent within 12 months. We assumed that those who re-enrolled within three 
months did so, on average, within 1.5 months, and we made similar assumptions about those who re-
enroll within six, nine, and 12 months. We assumed that the remaining 88.2 percent of disenrollees 
were disenrolled for the full year. We then relied on the NHEA projections (CMS, 2025d) to 
translate the projected enrollment losses to dollars and to project to future years. 
 
10 We assumed that enrollment and disenrollment happen on the first day of a given month, so disenrollment in a given month 
leads to loss of coverage for that full month.

45 
Federal Matching Assistance Percentage for Emergency 
Medicaid 
Federal Medicaid funds cannot be spent on services for certain immigrant groups, such as 
undocumented immigrants, non-immigrant visa holders, and lawfully present immigrants who have 
been resident in the United States for less than five years. However, states can receive federal matching 
funds for emergency medical services for members of these groups if they meet all the other state 
eligibility criteria for Medicaid. This is sometimes known as emergency Medicaid. 
After the ACA created the adult expansion group with a 90-percent federal match, some states 
began applying this enhanced FMAP to emergency care for any individual who met the requirements 
of the Medicaid ACA expansion group except for their immigration status. Sec. 71110 of OBBBA 
prohibits states from claiming the higher ACA matching rate for emergency Medicaid. Beginning in 
fiscal year 2027, states must use their base FMAP instead. 
To estimate the federal savings from this provision, we used data from MBES financial reports 
(CMS, undated-b). MBES data are based on state quarterly financial reports (CMS-64 forms) that 
contain a line item for “Emergency Services for Undocumented Aliens.” Although this line appears to 
refer only to undocumented immigrants, a recent CMS letter clarified that it applies to all emergency 
expenditures on aliens ineligible for full Medicaid benefits (CMS, 2025f). CBO used these data in a 
2024 response to a U.S. House of Representatives inquiry on total emergency Medicaid spending 
(Swagel, 2024).  
MBES contains information on total, state, and federal emergency Medicaid spending for each 
state. Federal spending reported in MBES represents a blend of spending at the base FMAP and 
spending at the enhanced ACA FMAP. We estimate federal spending under OBBBA by multiplying 
total spending by each state’s 2024 base FMAP (KFF, undated-a). To estimate the reduction in 
federal emergency Medicaid spending under OBBBA, we calculate the difference between this value 
and the federal spending reported in MBES. In some states (such as non-expansion states), there is no 
difference in federal spending because all spending occurs at the base FMAP. 
Verification of Enrollee Address Information 
OBBBA includes a provision that requires states to obtain updated enrollee address information 
using reliable data sources, such as the National Change of Address Database (U.S. Postal Service, 
undated). The provision directs the Secretary of Health and Human Services to establish a system for 
sharing these data across states, with the goal of reducing the incidence of individuals being enrolled in 
Medicaid in more than one state at once. 
We determined that this problem would primarily affect Medicaid managed care programs; 
simultaneous enrollment in more than one FFS program would presumably not induce additional 
demand for services, so the total cost of a given enrollee would remain the same. On the other hand, 
simultaneous enrollment in more than one managed care program would elicit managed care 
payments from the state to the MCO, regardless of use, leading to unnecessary spending by at least 
one state.

46 
We relied on a report published by OIG, which used data from CMS’s T-MSIS data, to identify 
individuals enrolled in Medicaid managed care programs in more than one state (OIG, 2022). The 
report provides state-level data on the number of beneficiaries in each state that are enrolled in 
another state and on the value of the capitation payments for those beneficiaries, for both August 
2019 and August 2020. Because Medicaid redeterminations were paused during the COVID-19 
public health emergency, thereby likely increasing the proportion of multistate enrollments, we rely on 
the August 2019 data only, taking it as a representative month. 
We assume that one-half of the beneficiaries concurrently enrolled in a given state are rightly 
enrolled in that state, and the other half reside in another state. Then, we calculate the proportion of 
simultaneously enrolled individuals per state and project that to future years using the NHEA 
enrollment projections. We also inflate the average value of the monthly capitation payments for 
simultaneously enrolled individuals as reported by OIG using the NHEA spending projections. We 
calculate forgone spending by the state using the average FMAP suggested by the MBES data, 
multiplied by projected per-capita cost of capitation payments times the projected number of 
incorrectly enrolled individuals per state. 
Good Faith Waiver Reduction for Erroneous Payments 
 OBBBA includes a provision that restricts federal reimbursement for improper Medicaid 
payments, such as payments for ineligible individuals, payments for ineligible services, overpayments, 
and payments that are questionable because of insufficient documentation. States are allowed by law 
to claim FMAP for improper payments of no more than 3 percent of total payments. However, CMS 
is permitted to waive the 3-percent maximum through what is known as a good faith waiver. Beginning 
in 2030, OBBBA will restrict use of the good faith waiver, allowing reimbursement to exceed the  
3-percent cap only for improper payments that are not related to eligibility.  
To model the changes to the good faith waiver, we obtained publicly available data from 
Medicaid’s PERM auditing process. PERM audits occur on a three-year cycle, with 17 states (and the 
District of Columbia) audited each year. To capture all states, we obtained data from 2022, 2023, and 
2024.  
PERM audits divide payments into two categories—those related to eligibility and those related 
to FFS, which capture errors in payments made to FFS providers (U.S. Government Accountability 
Office, 2025).11 We used the data from the PERM audits to calculate, for each state, the total 
improper payment and the maximum total improper payment amount that could be waived under the 
new rules. We assumed that errors related to FFS could be waived even if they pushed the total error 
rate above 3 percent and that errors related to eligibility could be waived only if they (in combination 
with other errors) were under 3 percent of total payments. For each state, we defined the maximum 
good faith payment reduction as the difference between the total improper payment and the maximum 
waivable amount under OBBBA rules. 
 
11 Technically, PERM audits include a third category for managed care errors, but because of the way this category is calculated, 
it is typically zero for all states (U.S. Government Accountability Office, 2025).

47 
Because we lacked data on the share of improper payments that CMS has historically waived for 
each state, we could not directly estimate the total payment reductions that would be expected under 
the new approach. Instead, we allocated the CBO’s estimate of $7.6 billion in federal savings across 
states based on the state’s maximum good faith payment reduction relative to the sum of maximum 
good faith payment reductions across all states. 
Immigrant Eligibility 
This OBBBA provision removes Medicaid eligibility for refugees, asylees, and individuals 
belonging to very specific populations, such as victims of human trafficking and their families. Data on 
populations other than refugees and asylees are not available, and because these groups are very small, 
we restrict our analysis to refugees and asylees. In addition, the measurement of uncertainty 
surrounding the estimated number of refugees and asylees is sufficiently large that any inclusion of 
these smaller populations would be effectively obscured by statistical noise, providing no meaningful 
analytical benefit. 
The key to modeling this provision is to understand the dynamics of status adjustment for these 
populations. The dynamic for refugees is shown in Figure A.1. 
Figure A.1. Refugee Dynamics 
 
We assume that on January 1 of year 𝑡, a cohort of 𝑁 refugees arrives in the United States. In 
accordance with federal law, refugees are required to apply for LPR status within one year of arrival; 
accordingly, we assume that all members of the cohort submit their LPR applications on January 1 of 
year 𝑡+ 1. We assume that all LPR applications are adjudicated within 12 to 24 months of 
submission, consistent with what was recently reported by the American Immigration Council, with 
decisions uniformly distributed across this period (Pinto and Coberly, 2025). Not all applications 
result in approval: A proportion 𝛾 of applicants successfully obtain LPR status; the remaining

and a share 1 −𝛾 exits the refugee population. The same proportions apply to the remaining half 
during the subsequent year, such that by January 1 of year 𝑡+ 3, all members of the cohort have been 
processed. Because our analysis is limited to individuals enrolled in Medicaid, the distinction between 
losing refugee status and transitioning to LPR status is immaterial. Consequently, the specific value of 
γ has no bearing on our results. As a result of these assumptions, our dynamic model is very 
straightforward: If a cohort of 𝑁 refugees arrives in year 𝑡, its size is 𝑁 in year 𝑡+ 1, 
!

and 0 in year 𝑡+ 3.  
Asylees are modeled in a similar manner. Although refugees must apply for LPR within one year 
of arrival, asylees are not required to apply and could, in principle, remain asylees indefinitely. 
Once the dynamic was established, the actual modeling of the provision was conceptually simple: 
• For each year and state, we summed over the cohort of active refugees and asylees to get the 
total number of individuals to which the provision applies. 
• We estimated how many of them are on Medicaid. 
• We estimated the average per capita medical expenditures for those on Medicaid. 
• We estimated the average FMAP for the state. 
• We applied the FMAP to estimate the savings to the federal and state government. 
To estimate the inflow of refugees and asylees and their socioeconomic composition, we used a 
variety of sources:  
• The Office of Homeland Security Statistics produces annual reports on the size, composition, 
and geographic distribution of refugees and asylees populations (Office of Homeland Security 
Statistics, 2025; Yap, 2025).  
• The Refugee Council USA (undated) publishes timely detailed statistics on refugee arrivals. 
• The Transactional Records Access Clearinghouse uses a transactional dataset to produce up-
to-date reports and statistics regarding immigration and other legally related topics 
(Transactional Records Access Clearinghouse, undated-b). Its asylum tool allows one to 
produce time series data by geography and asylee status (Transactional Records Access 
Clearinghouse, undated-a).  
• The 2022 Annual Survey of Refugees, which focuses on refugees who have been in the 
country less than five years, contains information about age, income, employment, health 
status, and insurance coverage of the refugee population (Office of Refugee Resettlement, 
2025). 
To project the populations of refugees and asylees up to 2034, we made the following 
assumptions: 
• The number of asylees remains constant at the current level. 
• The number of refugees in 2025 is capped at the amount reported in the first quarter of the 
year, since executive order “Realigning the United States Refugee Admissions Program” 
(White House, 2025) has completely halted the flow of refugees for the year.

49 
• The new ceiling for the number of refugees set by the administration is 7,500, and we assume 
that it starts in 2026 and continues until year 2034. 
To estimate average Medicaid expenditures for this population, we use a merged sample from the 
2022 and 2023 waves of the Medical Expenditure Panel Survey (MEPS) (Agency for Healthcare 
Research and Quality [AHRQ], 2023), reweighted to match the distribution of demographic and 
socioeconomic variables found in the Annual Survey of Refugees. We have found no evidence 
indicating that insured refugees use health care at different rates than the broader insured U.S. 
population, so no adjustment has been made for differential use. 
To estimate the federal and state portion of the savings, we have used state-specific average 
FMAPs derived from MBES (CMS, undated-b).  
The datasets we have described contain all the information needed to estimate the number of 
refugees and asylees who are expected to lose access to Medicaid in the 2025–2034 period.  
Cost-Sharing 
We group the health care services affected by the legislation into three broad categories: hospital 
inpatient care, hospital outpatient care, and office visits. Several types of services are explicitly 
excluded from the analysis, such as primary care visits, pregnancy- and childbirth-related services, and 
services related to mental health or substance use disorders. 
To estimate the copayments that states will collect, we applied the following procedure for each 
expansion state and each service category. 
• Estimate N: the number of expansion adults with incomes between 100 percent and 138 
percent of the FPL. 
• Estimate r: the average number of relevant services used per expansion adult. 
• Estimate y: an adjustment factor (less than one) that reflects the expected reduction in service 
use resulting from higher out-of-pocket costs; this factor depends on the price elasticity of 
demand for the service. 
• Calculate total copay revenue for the service category as C × r × N × y, where C is the 
copayment amount for that category. 
We implemented this procedure for each state beginning in 2028. We projected forward Medicaid 
expenditures for adults using a constant growth rate derived from NHEA projections for the 
Medicaid population (CMS, 2025d).  
We estimated the number of expansion adults with incomes between 100 percent and 138 percent 
of the FPL in each state using the micro-level datasets constructed for our analysis of work 
requirements, which are based on the 2023 ACS (U.S. Census Bureau, 2025). 
We estimate Medicaid per-capita use of outpatient services and office visits using the 2023 MEPS 
outpatient and office-based provider files (AHRQ, 2023). In both data sources, we exclude categories 
of care exempted from copayments under the legislation—specifically, emergency department visits 
and services related to mental health, pregnancy, and immunizations (AHRQ, 2023). A limitation of 
this approach is that the MEPS outpatient and office-based provider files do not support state-level 
analysis and are too small to identify Medicaid expansion adults with sufficient precision. Accordingly,

50 
we used these data only to estimate average use rates among Medicaid enrollees in the relevant age 
range, without distinguishing across states and without separately identifying expansion adults. 
Because the 2023 MEPS hospital inpatient file has an insufficient sample size for our purposes, 
hospital admissions per capita are instead drawn from a 2022 dataset (State Trends in Hospital 
Utilization by Payer), available through Healthcare Cost Utilization Project (HCUP) Fast Stats 
(AHRQ, undated). This dataset provides broad categories of hospitalizations, enabling us to remove 
stays associated with maternal health and mental health or substance use, both of which are exempt 
from cost-sharing. HCUP data are available for most Medicaid expansion states. For states without 
HCUP data, we imputed values using the average across states with data.  
To incorporate the expected reduction in use resulting from increased cost-sharing, we reviewed 
the literature to identify appropriate demand elasticity estimates. The most relevant evidence comes 
from Chandra, Gruber, and McKnight (2014), who examine the impact of copayments on health care 
spending among individuals below 300 percent of FPL in Massachusetts. They report elasticities of 
−0.115 for hospital spending, −0.203 for outpatient spending, and −0.147 for office-based care 
(Chandra, Gruber, and McKnight, 2014).  
Applying these elasticities presents a methodological challenge because the initial price for these 
services is zero, making standard elasticity-based calculations inapplicable. To address this issue, we 
proceeded as follows.  
Let 𝑄(𝑝) be the quantity of services demanded as a function of the price. From our data, we know 
𝑄(0) for each service. We impose a copay 𝑝‾, so we need to estimate the quantity 𝑄(𝑝‾). We know the 
elasticity of demand 𝜖, but we cannot apply it when the base price is 𝑝= 0. To estimate 𝑄(𝑝‾), we 
take advantage of the fact that 𝑝‾ is small and that a first-order expansion of the demand function 𝑄(𝑝) 
around 𝑝‾ is valid in the range [0, 𝑝‾]. Hence, we write the demand function as 
𝑄(𝑝) = 𝑄(𝑝‾) + 𝑄#(𝑝‾)(𝑝−𝑝‾) , ∀𝑝∈[0, 𝑝‾]. 
We now assume that the known elasticity 𝜖 is valid at the price 𝑝‾. By definition, we have

51 
Home- and Community-Based Services 
All states have at least one Medicaid HCBS waiver, enabling them to provide home-based care for 
people who have intellectual, physical, or developmental disabilities (Mohamed, Burns, and Watts, 
2025). These waivers often cover optional populations—for example, income eligibility limits might 
exceed mandatory requirements, and states can cap enrollment, leading to waiting lists (Saraswathula 
and Colello, 2025). 
OBBBA gives states greater flexibility to implement 1915(c) HCBS waivers (the most common 
type of HCBS waiver), enabling states to provide services for individuals who would not necessarily 
meet eligibility requirements for institutionalized care. Given the popularity of HCBS waivers, we 
expect that many states will be interested in these new flexibilities. However, the optional nature of 
HCBS waivers makes them a likely target for elimination or clawback to address budgetary pressures 
stemming from OBBBA’s other Medicaid cuts (Stark, 2025; Gonzales, 2025; Siddiqi, 2025). 
Given this budgetary pressure, we assume that only states that experience net increases to their 
general funds by 2034 because of OBBBA (e.g., resulting from such provisions as work requirements, 
which effectively force the state to reduce Medicaid eligibility) will make use of the additional 1915(c) 
flexibilities. We assume that states that experience net decreases to their general funds (e.g., because of 
provisions that reduce the ability to claim FMAP, such as the provider tax provisions) will not 
implement new HCBS waivers enabled by OBBBA. 
Because we did not have a reliable way to estimate what the new 1915(c) waivers will cost, we 
allocated CBO’s (2025c) estimate of $6.6 billion in increased federal spending. We started by 
allocating across all states with a net increase in general fund revenue because of OBBBA, using 
allocation amounts based on total Medicaid spending in each state. We then checked to see whether 
the state contribution necessary to support the assigned allocation would exceed the general fund 
increase from other OBBBA provisions. If so, we capped the HCBS allocation at the break-even level 
and reallocated the remainder across other states. We repeated this process iteratively until the total 
CBO estimate was allocated. We calculated state contributions based on their regular FMAPs.  
Rural Health Transformation Program 
Rural providers are disproportionately reliant on Medicaid reimbursement and face financial 
challenges because of low patient volumes, provider shortages, and lower average payment rates. To 
address these challenges and mitigate concerns that federal funding cuts to Medicaid could result in 
the closure of rural hospitals, OBBBA established the RHTP. Under this program, CMS will 
distribute $50 billion ($10 billion annually) between 2026 and 2030 to states that apply. States must 
use this funding on a set of 11 defined categories, such as interventions to reduce chronic illness, 
workforce retention and training, and creating data infrastructure. No more than 15 percent can be 
spent on provider payments, and these cannot be used to draw down additional federal funds. 
The RHTP funding formula uses a two-part allocation model consisting of baseline funding and 
workload funding. Baseline funding distributes one-half of the total funds ($25 billion) equally among 
all states with approved applications. The remaining $25 billion is allocated as workload funding, which

52 
depends on how states rank based on the quality of their proposed initiatives, their adoption of 
policies aligned with federal priorities, and data-based indicators reflecting the rurality of each state. 
Table A.2 summarizes the funding components, their weights in the overall formula (expressed as 
percentages), and whether each is determined by data metrics, state policy actions, or the quality of the 
state’s proposed initiatives. Some components incorporate more than one factor type. Each 
component of workload funding is scored on a continuous scale that is then converted to a percentile 
ranking. A state’s score for each category equals its percentile multiplied by that category’s weight. 
These weighted scores are normalized across all participating states, and final funding allocations are 
made proportionally to each state’s normalized score.  
To model how much funding would be allotted to each state, we assumed that all states would 
successfully apply for funding (excluding the District of Columbia, which is ineligible) and so would 
receive an equal share of the baseline funding. We cannot estimate a priori which states will have the 
strongest applications or will adopt the administration’s preferred policies, so we assigned all states the 
50th percentile for these categories. For the data-driven categories, we followed the methodology 
defined in CMS’s notice of funding opportunity (Grants.gov, undated), including its preferred data 
sources if possible.  
Calculating scores for each state required drawing on a broad variety of data sources. To identify 
rural populations, we combined tract-level population estimates from the 2019–2023 five-year ACS 
(U.S. Census Bureau, 2025) with the Health Resources and Services Administration’s (HRSA’s) 
September 2025 rural definition for census tracts (HRSA, 2025). We determined population in 
Frontier and Remote (FAR) ZIP codes by combining U.S. Department of Agriculture (2025) FAR 
data from the 2010 census with the 2023 Q1 ZIP-tract crosswalk from the Office of Policy 
Development and Research, U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development (undated), 
splitting a tract’s population equally when it spanned multiple ZIP codes. We also drew on 2010 
census data for state area measurements (U.S. Census Bureau, 2021). From the 2023 HCRIS data 
(CMS, 2025h), we identified hospitals that received Medicaid DSH payments and estimated 
uncompensated care (defined by CMS as charity care and bad debt [Asper, 2017]).

Component A.2 in Table A.2 requires tallying the number of rural hospitals and other rural 
health care facilities in each state using several different data sources. For rural hospitals, we combined 
data from the 2023 HCRIS (CMS, 2025h) with rural hospital designations developed by the Center

54 
for Healthcare Quality and Payment Reform (undated). We obtained rural health clinic counts from 
September 2025 Rural Health Clinic Enrollments data available from CMS (2025i). We used the 
September 2025 HRSA (undated) database on health center service delivery and look-alike sites to 
count federally qualified health centers. We filtered to active sites only and counted centers rather 
than sites. For community mental health centers and certified community behavioral health clinics, we 
used the Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration’s National Directory of 
Mental Health Treatment Facilities (Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration, 
2024). We obtained counts of opioid treatment providers from September 2025 Opioid Treatment 
Program Provider Data available from CMS (2025g), though this source includes only providers 
registered with Medicare and might therefore undercount facilities in some states. Other mental 
health and opioid treatment provider datasets were defined at the ZIP code level. We determined 
whether these providers were rural using the 2023 Q1 ZIP-tract crosswalk (Office of Policy 
Development and Research, U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development, undated), 
labeling a ZIP code as rural if most of its businesses were in rural tracts.

Appendix B 
Detailed Results Tables 
In Tables B.1 through B.13, we list the detailed, state-level Medicaid budget impacts and general 
fund impacts of the provisions we studied both overall (Tables B.1 and B.2) and by provision (Tables 
B.3–B.13). We provide the cumulative impact by 2028, 2030, and 2034, and we present percentage 
changes in budgets as a percentage of the total state Medicaid budget (inclusive of both state and 
federal contributions). However, in Table B.2, we present the overall state general fund impacts as a 
percentage of the total state Medicaid budget and as a percentage of the state portion only. Table B.14 
provides the state-level relative importance of each provision in terms of impact on state Medicaid 
budget. Table B.15 details the enrollment impacts, by provision, in 2034.
